%\subsubsection{Drift under the jumping num\'eraire measure}


\subsection{Covariance integrals}\label{ssec:covariance_integrals}

\subsubsection{Introduction}
The expectation and covariance matrix necessary for simulation, as well as all the convexity adjustments in the model, reduce to sums of integrals of the type:
\begin{eqnarray}
  I^{a,b}_{t,S;T_a,T_b} &=& \int_t^S \Sigma^a_{u,T_a} \cdot \Sigma^b_{u,T_b} \, du
  ~=~ \sum_{j_a=1}^{N_a} \sum_{j_b=1}^{N_b} \int_t^S \rho^{(a,j_a),(b,j_b)}_u \sigma^{a,j_a}_u  \sigma^{b,j_b}_u G^{a,j_a}_{u,T_a} G^{b,j_b}_{u,T_b} \, du
\end{eqnarray}
where we set $G^{x}_{u,T} = 1$ for the FX factor. The simulation also requires the same integrals with $G^{a,j}_{u,T}$ replaced by $M^{a,j}_{u,T}$. So we consider the elementary integrals
\begin{eqnarray}
  J_{t,S}\left[\ell^{a,i},\ell^{b,j}\right] &=& \int_t^S \rho^{(a,i),(b,j)}_u \sigma^{a,i}_u  \sigma^{b,j}_u \, \ell^{a,i}_u \, \ell^{b,j}_u \, du
\end{eqnarray}
where each \emph{loading} $\ell^{a,i}_u$ is one of $M^{a,i}_{u,T}$, $G^{a,i}_{u,T}$ or $1$, for some maturity $T\ge S$. Sums of these give all the integrals we need. For example, $\Sigma^a_{u,T;\theta}$ has loadings $G^{a,i}_{u,T+\theta} - G^{a,i}_{u,T}$, and $y^{a,b}_t$ has entries $J_{0,t}[M^{a,i}_{\cdot,t},M^{b,j}_{\cdot,t}]$. For vector loadings $\Lambda_u = \sum_{(a,i)} c_{a,i}\,\sigma^{a,i}_u\ell^{a,i}_u\,\widetilde{e}_{(a,i)}$, we write $J_{t,S}[\Lambda,\Lambda']$ for the corresponding bilinear combination.

\subsubsection{Piecewise constant parameters}
We assume the spot vols, correlations and mean reversion parameters are piecewise constant on a grid $(t_k)$. On a segment $[t_k,t_{k+1}]$ of length $\Delta_k$, we write $\beta = \beta^{a,i}_k$ and $s = t_{k+1}-u \in [0,\Delta_k]$, and introduce
\begin{eqnarray}
  \varphi_\beta(s) ~=~ \int_0^s e^{-\beta v}\,dv ~=~ \frac{1-e^{-\beta s}}{\beta} \qquad (\varphi_0(s) = s).
\end{eqnarray}

\begin{lemm}\label{lemm:affine_loadings}
For $u\in[t_k,t_{k+1}]$ and $T\ge t_{k+1}$, every loading is affine in $\varphi_\beta(t_{k+1}-u)$:
\begin{eqnarray}
  \ell_u &=& p + q\,\varphi_\beta(t_{k+1}-u)
\end{eqnarray}
with
\begin{eqnarray*}
  (p,q) &=& \left( M_{t_{k+1},T}\, ,\, -\beta M_{t_{k+1},T} \right) \quad \textrm{for $\ell = M_{\cdot,T}$,} \\
  (p,q) &=& \left( G_{t_{k+1},T}\, ,\, 1-\beta G_{t_{k+1},T} \right) \quad \textrm{for $\ell = G_{\cdot,T}$,} \\
  (p,q) &=& (1,0) \quad \textrm{for $\ell = 1$.}
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{lemm}

\begin{proof}
We have $M_{u,T} = e^{-\beta s} M_{t_{k+1},T}$ and $G_{u,T} = \varphi_\beta(s) + e^{-\beta s}G_{t_{k+1},T}$, and $e^{-\beta s} = 1-\beta\varphi_\beta(s)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}[Covariance integrals]\label{prop:covariance_integrals}
With the notations of \autoref{lemm:affine_loadings}, for loadings $\ell^{a,i}$ and $\ell^{b,j}$ with coefficients $(p_a,q_a)$, $(p_b,q_b)$ and mean reversions $\alpha = \beta^{a,i}_k$, $\beta = \beta^{b,j}_k$ on the segment,
\begin{eqnarray}
  J_{t_k,t_{k+1}}\left[\ell^{a,i},\ell^{b,j}\right] &=& \rho_k \sigma^{a,i}_k\sigma^{b,j}_k \left( p_ap_b\Delta_k + p_aq_b\Phi_1(\beta) + q_ap_b\Phi_1(\alpha) + q_aq_b\Phi_2(\alpha,\beta) \right)
\end{eqnarray}
where
\begin{eqnarray}
  \Phi_1(\beta) &=& \int_0^{\Delta}\varphi_\beta(s)\,ds ~=~ \frac{\Delta - \varphi_\beta(\Delta)}{\beta} , \\
  \Phi_2(\alpha,\beta) &=& \int_0^{\Delta}\varphi_\alpha(s)\varphi_\beta(s)\,ds ~=~ \frac{\Delta - \varphi_\alpha(\Delta) - \varphi_\beta(\Delta) + \varphi_{\alpha+\beta}(\Delta)}{\alpha\beta} .
\end{eqnarray}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Expand the product of the two affine loadings and use $\varphi_\alpha\varphi_\beta = (1-e^{-\alpha s}-e^{-\beta s}+e^{-(\alpha+\beta)s})/(\alpha\beta)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Stable evaluation]
The singularities of $\Phi_1$ and $\Phi_2$ at zero mean reversion are removable but the closed forms lose precision near them. Zero mean reversion is the Ho-Lee case and does occur in practice, for example for the FX factor or a level factor. Both functions are entire:
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \Phi_1(\beta) ~=~ \sum_{n\ge 0} \frac{(-\beta)^n\Delta^{n+2}}{(n+2)!} , \qquad
  \Phi_2(\alpha,\beta) ~=~ \sum_{m,n\ge 0} \frac{(-\alpha)^m(-\beta)^n\Delta^{m+n+3}}{(m+1)!\,(n+1)!\,(m+n+3)} .
\end{eqnarray*}
We use these series when $|\beta|\Delta < 0.1$ (resp. $\max(|\alpha|,|\beta|)\Delta < 0.5$) and the closed forms, computed with \texttt{expm1}, when $\min(|\alpha|,|\beta|,|\alpha+\beta|)\Delta > 0.05$. In the remaining mixed case, where one rate is small and the other is not, we integrate $\varphi_\alpha\varphi_\beta$ by 64-point Gauss-Legendre quadrature. The integrand is entire, so this is exact to machine precision for the values of $|\beta|\Delta$ met in practice.
\end{rema}

\begin{algo}[Covariance integrals]\label{algo:covariance_integrals}
Given the parameter grid $(t_k)$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Precompute, for each factor, $\int_0^{t_k}\beta_v dv$ on the grid. Then $M_{t,T} = e^{-\int_t^T\beta_v dv}$ for any $t$, $T$.
\item Compute $G_{t_{k},T}$ for all grid points $t_k\le T$ by the backward recursion
\begin{eqnarray*}
  G_{t_k,T} &=& \varphi_{\beta_k}(\Delta_k) + e^{-\beta_k \Delta_k}\, G_{t_{k+1},T} ,
\end{eqnarray*}
starting from the segment containing $T$. It avoids the representation $G_{t,T} = (\Lambda_T - \Lambda_t)/h_t$ with $\Lambda_T = G_{0,T}$, whose difference of two large numbers can lose precision when $t$ is large or the mean reversion is negative.
\item Split $[t,S]$ at the grid points. On each segment compute $(p,q)$ for both loadings by \autoref{lemm:affine_loadings} and the contribution by \autoref{prop:covariance_integrals}. Sum the contributions.
\end{enumerate}
The cost is $O(K)$ per pair of factors, where $K$ is the number of segments in $[t,S]$, i.e. $O(K N_a N_b)$ for $I^{a,b}_{t,S;T_a,T_b}$. All quantities are closed form.
\end{algo}

\begin{rema}[Numerical verification]
We compared \autoref{algo:covariance_integrals} to adaptive quadrature in which $G$ itself is computed by quadrature of $M$. The test used $84$ combinations of loadings ($M$, $G$, $1$), sub-intervals not aligned on the grid and maturities up to $12$Y, with one factor having zero and $10^{-7}$ mean reversion and one factor negative mean reversion on some segments. The maximum relative error is $7\cdot 10^{-16}$. The kernels $\Phi_1$, $\Phi_2$ alone agree with quadrature to $4\cdot 10^{-16}$, including the cases $\alpha=0$, $\alpha = 10^{-9}$, $\alpha+\beta = 0$ and $\beta\Delta = 10$.
\end{rema}


\subsection{Convexity adjustments}
All collateral convexity and quanto adjustments of the deterministic volatility section are exponentials of covariance integrals, computed by \autoref{algo:covariance_integrals}. For example:
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \ln \frac{x^{df|j}_{t,T}}{x^{df|i}_{t,T}} &=& -\int_t^T \Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\cdot\Sigma^{i\to j}_{u,T}\,du , \\
  \ln \frac{1+\theta L^{e|j}_{t,T;\theta}}{1+\theta L^{e|i}_{t,T;\theta}} &=& -\int_t^T \Sigma^{e|e}_{u,T;\theta}\cdot\Sigma^{i\to j}_{u,T+\theta}\,du ,
\end{eqnarray*}
where $\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}$ has loadings $G^{d}_{u,T}$, $-G^f_{u,T}$, $-G^c_{u,T}$ and $1$, and $\Sigma^{e|e}_{u,T;\theta}$ has loadings $G^e_{u,T+\theta} - G^e_{u,T}$.

The coupon convexities of \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} are sums of covariance integrals too. The continuous one \eqref{eq:coupon_convexity_continuous} needs three. The daily one \eqref{eq:coupon_convexity_daily} has one term per overnight fixing ($92$ for a quarterly coupon with $\delta = 1/365$: $91$ full steps and a shortened last one), each with loadings $\Sigma^{f,i}_{u,t_{k+1}}-\Sigma^{f,i}_{u,t_k} = \sigma^{f,i}_uM^{f,i}_{u,t_k}G^{f,i}_{t_k,t_{k+1}}$. With $a' = \max(t,a)$ and the cocycle $M^{f,i}_{u,t_k} = M^{f,i}_{u,a'}M^{f,i}_{a',t_k}$, it is
\begin{eqnarray*}
  -\ln K^\delta_t(a,b) &=& \sum_{i\in f,\,j\in c}\ \sum_{k:\,t_k>t} G^{f,i}_{t_k,t_{k+1}}M^{f,i}_{a',t_k}\left( J_{t,a'}[M^{f,i}_{\cdot,a'},G^{c,j}_{\cdot,b}] + J_{a',t_k}[M^{f,i}_{\cdot,a'},G^{c,j}_{\cdot,b}] \right) ,
\end{eqnarray*}
one covariance integral over $[t,a']$ per pair of factors, plus the cumulative integrals $J_{a',t_k}$ on the grid refined by the knots, accumulated in closed form with the loadings of \autoref{lemm:affine_loadings} (\texttt{market.compounded\_convexity}; the loading $M^{f,i}_{u,a'} = 1/M^{f,i}_{a',u}$ for $u>a'$ is still affine in $\varphi_\beta$). This takes $0.43$ms for a daily quarterly coupon, against $48$ms for the loop over fixings (\texttt{\_compounded\_convexity\_scalar}, kept as the test oracle); the two agree to $|\Delta\ln K|\le1.1\cdot10^{-16}$.

\begin{rema}[Verification of the coupon convexities]\label{rema:coupon_convexity_verification}
The closed forms of \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} were checked on the test model (function \texttt{default\_model} of \texttt{lgcm.py}: two domestic factors, one foreign, one collateral basis factor, $\rho^{f,c} = -0.4$, piecewise constant parameters) against an independent benchmark (function \texttt{coupon\_expectation\_exact} of \texttt{benchmarks.py}), which computes $\EE^{f|d,b}_t[C]$ as an exact Gaussian expectation: the state is augmented with the integrated short rates and with $\ln C$, propagated with the exact transitions of \autoref{prop:lgcm_transition_rn} from fixing to fixing ($\ln C$ accumulates $-\ln Z^{f|f}_{t_k,t_{k+1}}$, an affine function of the state, at each fixing), and the deflated payoff is the exponential of an affine function of a Gaussian vector. It does not use the covariance kernels of the closed forms.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Over Libor, daily steps $1/52$ and $1/12$, $t\in\{0, 2, 5+5/52\}$ (the last one inside a period) and $a\in\{5,10,20\}$, $|\ln K_{\mathrm{closed}}-\ln K_{\mathrm{bench}}|\le4.4\cdot10^{-16}$, for $\ln K$ of order $5\cdot10^{-5}$. The benchmark under native collateral returns $Z^{f|f}_{t,a}/Z^{f|f}_{t,b}$ to $2.2\cdot10^{-16}$ (\autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward}), and $K$ does not depend on the state $z_t$ (to $10^{-12}$). The Libor formula equals the one-step daily formula to $10^{-14}$, and $\ln(K^0/K^{\mathrm{L}})$ equals the in-arrears integral of \autoref{prop:coupon_continuous_error} to $10^{-12}$ (relative).
\item Monte Carlo (one quarterly period $[5,5.25]$, daily step $1/52$, $2^{16}$ paths, seed $13$): $z = -0.36$ for $K^\delta$, and $K = 1$ is rejected at $33.4$ standard errors (s.e.\ $1.47\cdot10^{-6}$ on $K$).
\item Size of the in-arrears term (numerical observation, quarterly periods seen from $t=0$): $K-1$ is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
$a$ & Libor & daily ($1/365$) & continuous & $\ln(K^0/K^{\mathrm{L}})$ \\
\hline
$2$  & $9.956\cdot10^{-6}$ & $1.0039\cdot10^{-5}$ & $1.0040\cdot10^{-5}$ & $8.4\cdot10^{-8}$ \\
$10$ & $1.7963\cdot10^{-4}$ & $1.7975\cdot10^{-4}$ & $1.7975\cdot10^{-4}$ & $1.14\cdot10^{-7}$ \\
$20$ & $5.4888\cdot10^{-4}$ & $5.4899\cdot10^{-4}$ & $5.4899\cdot10^{-4}$ & $1.11\cdot10^{-7}$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The in-arrears term is about $10^{-7}$ and roughly constant in $a$, as \autoref{prop:coupon_continuous_error} predicts (bound $\bar{\sigma}^f\bar{\sigma}^c\theta^3/3\approx3.9\cdot10^{-7}$ with $\bar{\sigma}^f\approx1.47\%$, $\bar{\sigma}^c\approx0.51\%$, the largest spot volatilities), while the Libor term grows with $a$: the in-arrears term is $0.8\%$ of the convexity at $a = 2$ and $0.06\%$ at $a = 10$. A Monte Carlo cannot resolve it: in item 2 it is $0.07$ standard errors (about $2^{28}$ paths would be needed for $4$ standard errors). The exact benchmark of item 1 resolves it ($10^{-7}$ against $4.4\cdot10^{-16}$).
\item Continuous vs daily (numerical observation, $a = 10$): $|\ln(K^\delta/K^0)| = 1.380\cdot10^{-8}$, $6.771\cdot10^{-9}$, $3.343\cdot10^{-9}$, $1.661\cdot10^{-9}$ for $\delta = 1/26, 1/52, 1/104, 1/208$ (ratios $2.04$, $2.03$, $2.01$) and $9.44\cdot10^{-10}$ for $\delta = 1/365$. The ratio of $\ln(K^\delta/K^0)$ to the leading term \eqref{eq:coupon_continuous_leading} is $1.0256$, $1.0128$, $1.0064$, $1.0036$, $1.0018$ for $\delta = 1/52, 1/104, 1/208, 1/365, 1/730$ (the same at $a = 2$ and $a = 20$ to $10^{-3}$): the first order term is confirmed and the remainder is $O(\delta^2)$ relative to $\delta$, as proved. In these models the continuous coupon is within $10^{-9}$ of the daily one on $\ln K$.
\end{enumerate}
The tests are in \texttt{tests/test\_ois.py}.
\end{rema}


\subsection{CurveEngine implementation}

In practice, all these computations can be abstracted in a helper class.

\subsubsection{Reconstruction formulas}



\newpage%
\subsection{Monte Carlo simulation}\label{ssec:lgcm_simulation}

In the LGCM, $y^{a,b}_t$ and $v^{f,c}_t$ are deterministic. They are precomputed as $y^{(a,i),(b,j)}_t = J_{0,t}[M^{a,i}_{\cdot,t},M^{b,j}_{\cdot,t}]$ and $v^{(f,i)}_t = \sum_{j} J_{0,t}[M^{f,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{c,j}_{\cdot,t}]$. The state $z_t = [z^d_t | z^f_t | z^c_t | z^x_t]$ of \autoref{theo:qgcm} is Gaussian, and its transition between two dates $s<t$ can be sampled exactly, whatever the step size. For a vector loading $\Lambda$, we write $J[\Lambda,\Lambda'] = J_{s,t}[\Lambda,\Lambda']$ and use the loadings
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \Sigma^a_{\cdot,t}: \ \left(G^{a,j}_{\cdot,t}\right)_j , \qquad
  \Sigma^{df}_{\cdot,t} = \Sigma^d_{\cdot,t} - \Sigma^f_{\cdot,t} - \Sigma^c_{\cdot,t} + \sigma^{df} , \qquad
  \hat{\Sigma}_{\cdot,t} = \Sigma^f_{\cdot,t} + \Sigma^c_{\cdot,t} = \Sigma^{f|d}_{\cdot,t} .
\end{eqnarray*}

\subsubsection{Transition and covariance matrix}

\begin{prop}[Exact transition under the risk-neutral measure]\label{prop:lgcm_transition_rn}
Let $s<t$, $G^a = G^a_{s,t}$ and $\hat{G} = [G^f;G^c]$. Under $\QQ^{d,\RN}$, conditionally on $\mathcal{F}_s$,
\begin{eqnarray*}
  z^a_t &=& \mathcal{M}^a_{s,t}z^a_s + \mu^a_{s,t} + \varepsilon^a , \qquad a\in\{d,f,c\} \\
  z^x_t &=& z^x_s + G^{d,\top}z^d_s - \hat{G}^\top\hat{z}_s + \mu^x_{s,t} + \varepsilon^x \\
  \int_s^t r^{d|d}_u\,du &=& -\ln Z^{d|d}_{0,s,t} + G^{d,\top}z^d_s + \mu^r_{s,t} + \varepsilon^r \\
  \int_s^t q^{f\to d}_u\,du &=& -\ln \QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,s,t} + G^{c,\top}z^c_s + G^{f,\top} v^{f,c}_s + \mu^q_{s,t} + \varepsilon^q
\end{eqnarray*}
with $\hat{z}_s = [z^f_s + v^{f,c}_s ; z^c_s]$, deterministic means
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \mu^{d,i}_{s,t} &=& \textstyle\sum_{j} J[M^{d,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{d,j}_{\cdot,t}] + M^{d,i}_{s,t}\left(y^{d,d}_sG^d\right)_i \\
  \mu^{f,i}_{s,t} &=& \textstyle\sum_{j} J[M^{f,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{f,j}_{\cdot,t}] + M^{f,i}_{s,t}\left(y^{f,f}_sG^f\right)_i - J[M^{f,i}_{\cdot,t},1^x] \\
  \mu^{c,i}_{s,t} &=& \textstyle\sum_{j} J[M^{c,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{c,j}_{\cdot,t}] + \sum_{j} J[M^{c,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{f,j}_{\cdot,t}] + M^{c,i}_{s,t}\left(y^{c,c}_sG^c + y^{c,f}_sG^f\right)_i - J[M^{c,i}_{\cdot,t},1^x] \\
  \mu^x_{s,t} &=& \frac{1}{2}G^{d,\top}y^{d,d}_sG^d - \frac{1}{2}\hat{G}^\top\hat{y}_s\hat{G} - \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^{df}_{\cdot,t},\Sigma^{df}_{\cdot,t}] + J[\Sigma^{df}_{\cdot,t},\Sigma^{d}_{\cdot,t}] \\
  \mu^r_{s,t} &=& \frac{1}{2}G^{d,\top}y^{d,d}_sG^d + \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^{d}_{\cdot,t},\Sigma^{d}_{\cdot,t}] \\
  \mu^q_{s,t} &=& \frac{1}{2}G^{c,\top}y^{c,c}_sG^c + G^{c,\top}y^{c,f}_sG^f + \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^{c}_{\cdot,t},\Sigma^{c}_{\cdot,t}] + J[\Sigma^{f}_{\cdot,t},\Sigma^{c}_{\cdot,t}] - J[\Sigma^{c}_{\cdot,t},\sigma^{df}]
\end{eqnarray*}
(where $1^x$ denotes the unit loading of the FX factor) and a centred Gaussian noise
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \varepsilon^{a,i} = \int_s^t \sigma^{a,i}_u M^{a,i}_{u,t} \, d\widetilde{W}^{(a,i)}_u , \quad
  \varepsilon^x = \int_s^t \Sigma^{df}_{u,t}\cdot dW_u , \quad
  \varepsilon^r = \int_s^t \Sigma^{d}_{u,t}\cdot dW_u , \quad
  \varepsilon^q = \int_s^t \Sigma^{c}_{u,t}\cdot dW_u ,
\end{eqnarray*}
independent of $\mathcal{F}_s$. Its covariance matrix is $C_{s,t} = \left(J[\Lambda_k,\Lambda_l]\right)_{k,l}$, where $\Lambda_k$ runs over the loadings of the noises: $M^{a,i}_{\cdot,t}$, $\Sigma^{df}_{\cdot,t}$, $\Sigma^d_{\cdot,t}$ and $\Sigma^c_{\cdot,t}$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Rates factors.} Integrating the dynamic of \autoref{theo:qgcm}, $z^a_t = \mathcal{M}^a_{s,t}z^a_s + \int_s^t\mathcal{M}^a_{u,t}(\textrm{drift}^a_u\,du + \sigma^{a,\top}_{z,u}dW_u)$. For the term $y^{a,b}_u\vun$ of the drift, Fubini gives
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \int_s^t \mathcal{M}^a_{u,t}y^{a,b}_u\vun\,du ~=~ \int_s^t \mathcal{M}^a_{w,t}\sigma^{a,\top}_{z,w}\sigma^b_{z,w}G^b_{w,t}\,dw + \mathcal{M}^a_{s,t}y^{a,b}_s G^b_{s,t} ,
\end{eqnarray*}
and $\int_s^t\mathcal{M}^a_{u,t}\sigma^{a,\top}_{z,u}\sigma^{df}_u\,du$ gives the terms $J[M^{a,i}_{\cdot,t},1^x]$.

\emph{FX.} The FX forward $x^{df|d}_{u,t}$ is a lognormal $\QQ^{d|d,t}$-martingale with volatility $\Sigma^{df}_{u,t}$ and $x^{df|d}_{t,t} = x^{df}_t$. So $\ln x^{df}_t = \ln x^{df|d}_{s,t} - \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^{df},\Sigma^{df}] + \int_s^t \Sigma^{df}_{u,t}\cdot dW^{d|d,t}_u$. We express $x^{df|d}_{s,t} = x^{df}_sZ^{f|d}_{s,t}/Z^{d|d}_{s,t}$ with the reconstruction formulas and use $dW^{d|d,t}_u = dW_u + \Sigma^d_{u,t}du$.

\emph{Numeraire.} Integrating $d\ln Z^{d|d}_{u,t} = (r^{d|d}_u - \frac{1}{2}|\Sigma^d_{u,t}|^2)du - \Sigma^d_{u,t}\cdot dW_u$ from $s$ to $t$ gives $\int_s^tr^{d|d}_u du = -\ln Z^{d|d}_{s,t} + \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^d,\Sigma^d] + \varepsilon^r$, and we conclude with the reconstruction formula. The same argument applied to $Z^{f|d}_{u,t}$ and $Z^{f|f}_{u,t}$ under $\QQ^{f,\RN}$, with $dW^{f,\RN} = dW - \sigma^{df}du$, gives $\int_s^t q^{f\to d}_u\,du$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}
The covariance matrix $C_{s,t}$ does not depend on the measure since all changes of measure we consider have deterministic Girsanov kernels. It is computed once per time step and its Cholesky (or eigenvalue) decomposition is stored. The state is then simulated as $X_t = F_{s,t}X_s + \mu_{s,t} + C_{s,t}^{1/2}\xi$ with $\xi\sim N(0,I)$. Only the components needed by the chosen num\'eraire are simulated: $\int r^{d|d}$ under $\QQ^{d,\RN}$, nothing under the forward and jumping num\'eraire measures.
\end{rema}

\subsubsection{Drifts under the risk-neutral, $T$-forward and jumping num\'eraire measures}

\begin{prop}[Change of num\'eraire]\label{prop:lgcm_drifts}
Let $\varepsilon^k = \int_s^t\Lambda_{k,u}\cdot dW_u$ be any of the noises of \autoref{prop:lgcm_transition_rn}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Under the domestic $T$-forward measure $\QQ^{d|d,T}$, $T\ge t$, the transition is the same with means
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \mu^{k,T}_{s,t} &=& \mu^{k}_{s,t} - J[\Lambda_k, \Sigma^d_{\cdot,T}]
\end{eqnarray*}
and the same covariance matrix. For example, for $T=t$ the domestic rates means reduce to $\mu^{d,t}_{s,t} = \mathcal{M}^d_{s,t}y^{d,d}_sG^d_{s,t}$, and the FX mean to $\frac{1}{2}G^{d,\top}y^{d,d}_sG^d - \frac{1}{2}\hat{G}^\top\hat{y}_s\hat{G} - \frac{1}{2}J[\Sigma^{df},\Sigma^{df}]$.
\item The jumping num\'eraire associated with a tenor structure $T_0 = 0 < T_1 < \ldots$ is
\begin{eqnarray}
  N_t &=& Z^{d|d}_{t,T_{q(t)+1}} \prod_{k=0}^{q(t)} \frac{1}{Z^{d|d}_{T_k,T_{k+1}}} , \qquad q(t) = \max\{k : T_k\le t\} .
\end{eqnarray}
It corresponds to rolling an investment in the zero coupon bond maturing at the next tenor date. On $[T_k,T_{k+1}]$ the associated measure $\QQ^{d,J}$ coincides with the $T_{k+1}$-forward measure. So, when the simulation grid is contained in the tenor structure, the means of a step $[t_k,t_{k+1}]$ under $\QQ^{d,J}$ are given by 1.\ with $T = t_{k+1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
1. The Girsanov kernel is deterministic: $dW^{d|d,T}_u = dW_u + \Sigma^d_{u,T}du$, so $\varepsilon^k = \int_s^t\Lambda_{k,u}\cdot dW^{d|d,T}_u - J[\Lambda_k,\Sigma^d_{\cdot,T}]$. For the domestic factors with $T=t$, the term $\sum_j J[M^{d,i}_{\cdot,t},G^{d,j}_{\cdot,t}]$ cancels with the shift. For the FX, the shift cancels the last term of $\mu^x$.

2. $N$ is continuous at the tenor dates. Between $T_k$ and $T_{k+1}$ it is proportional to $Z^{d|d}_{t,T_{k+1}}$, with an $\mathcal{F}_{T_k}$-measurable factor, so the density of the measure change is that of the $T_{k+1}$-forward measure on that interval.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}
The jumping num\'eraire has several advantages:
\begin{itemize}
\item The num\'eraire update $N_{T_{k+1}} = N_{T_k}/Z^{d|d}_{T_k,T_{k+1}}$ only requires the reconstruction formula at $T_k$, whereas $\QQ^{d,\RN}$ requires the simulation of $\int r^{d|d}_u\,du$.
\item On each step, the means are the simplest ones: the domestic factors only carry $\mathcal{M}^d_{s,t}y^{d,d}_sG^d_{s,t}$, and the log FX spot is the log FX forward $\ln x^{df|d}_{s,t}$, lognormally corrected.
\end{itemize}
An earlier version of the formula for $N_t$ used $Z_{t,T_{q(t)}}$, which is not defined since $T_{q(t)}\le t$.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Numerical verification]
We propagated the exact Gaussian law of the state through a quarterly grid up to $3$Y with the transitions above and the model of the verification of \autoref{theo:qgcm}. Under $\QQ^{d,\RN}$, the mean and covariance agree with those obtained by integrating the moment ODEs of \autoref{theo:qgcm} to $4\cdot 10^{-17}$ and $8\cdot 10^{-17}$ (absolute). The martingale identities for $Z^{d|d}_{\cdot,T}$, $x^{df}Z^{f|d}_{\cdot,T}$ and $x^{df}Z^{f|f}_{\cdot,T}$ (under $\QQ^{d,\RN}$), for $Z^{d|d}_{\cdot,T}/Z^{d|d}_{\cdot,T^*}$ and the FX forward (under $\QQ^{d|d,T^*}$, $T^* = 20$Y), and for $Z^{d|d}_{\cdot,T}/N$ and $x^{df}Z^{f|d}_{\cdot,T}/N$ (under $\QQ^{d,J}$) all hold to machine precision. The measure shifts are not small: the means of the simulated variables at $3$Y differ by up to $1.4\cdot 10^{-4}$ between $\QQ^{d,\RN}$ and $\QQ^{d,J}$, and $1.4\cdot 10^{-2}$ between $\QQ^{d,\RN}$ and $\QQ^{d|d,T^*}$.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Initial curves]\label{rema:simulation_initial_curves}
The transitions only involve the initial curves through $Z_{0,s,t}$ at simulation dates and $Z_{0,t,T}$ at payment dates. The instantaneous forward curve $r_{0,\cdot}$ never needs to be evaluated pointwise. In particular, the discontinuities of $r_{0,\cdot}$ created by piecewise flat interpolation have no effect on the simulation (see \autoref{ssec:interpolation_consistency}).
\end{rema}



\newpage%
\section{The Linear Gaussian Collateral Model: Calibration}

\setcounter{subsection}{-1}
\subsection{Introduction}

\subsubsection{Single currency market}%
The model incorporates a Linear Gaussian Model for each currency. This sub-model is meant to price instruments with native collateral.

In practice, this means that for each currency, we have an embedded single currency LGM which can be calibrated independently to its native IR market:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Native discount $Z^{x|x}_{t,T}$  and index curves  $L^{x|x}_{t,T;\theta}$ can be built by any standard method (independently of volatilities\footnote{Spot volatilities are included in Future convexity adjustments but they do not require a very precise calibration of the volatility so these convexity adjustments are typically marked externally if not hardcoded. Conversely, the values of the futures in the curve building have almost no impact on the volatilities calibrated to swaption vols.}).
\item Volatilities $\sigma^{x|x}_{t,T}$  can be bootstrapped on the fly.
\item Mean reversions can be marked/calibrated externally as usual to match other ATM vols or, in the multi-dimensional case, some delivered rates correlations.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Cross-currency market}%
The next step is to calibrate to the cross-currency market. We focus on the G10 currencies where liquid instruments are
\begin{enumerate}
\item FX forwards (for short maturities)
\item Resetting currency basis swaps
\item FX options
\end{enumerate}
We will assume that they are collateralised in currency $d$ (USD in practice).

\subsubsection{Calibration targets and marked inputs}%
The calibration described in this section, and implemented in \texttt{calibration.py} of the numerical companion, takes the following quotes:
\begin{enumerate}
\item native swap rates in $d$ and $f$: OIS swaps, whose floating leg pays compounded overnight (RFR) coupons, or Libor swaps with a constant funding spread;
\item the FX spot $x^{df}_0$ and $d$-collateralised FX forwards (outrights or points), in the direct quotation ($x^{df}$) or the inverted one ($1/x^{df}$);
\item spot starting resetting currency basis (CB) swap spreads, $d$-collateralised, whose legs pay Libor or compounded overnight coupons;
\item ATM normal volatilities of native swaptions in $d$ and $f$;
\item ATM Black volatilities of FX options;
\item ATM normal volatilities of forward resetting CB spreads (implied, or historical estimates when there is no option market).
\end{enumerate}
The following inputs are marked, not calibrated: the mean reversions $\beta^{a,i}$, the correlation matrix $\Omega$, the native funding spreads $\kappa^e$ and, within each block $a\in\{d,f,c\}$, fixed factor weights $w^{a,i}$. The spot volatilities are parametrised as
\begin{eqnarray}
  \sigma^{a,i}_t &=& \lambda^a_t\, w^{a,i} , \qquad a\in\{d,f,c\},
\end{eqnarray}
where the scalar level $\lambda^a_t$ and the FX spot volatility $\sigma^x_t$ are piecewise constant on the expiries of the options that calibrate them, with flat extrapolation after the last expiry. The model knots are the union of all expiries. Calibrating one level per block with fixed weights is a design choice: it gives one unknown per option quote, and it keeps the shape of the factor structure that was marked. If a block has no option quotes (e.g.\ no CB volatilities), its level is marked.

The companion has three example markets (\texttt{market\_data.example\_quotes}), all with USD $= d$, SOFR collateral and SOFR domestic coupons: EURUSD (the original example, EUR $= f$ with ESTR collateral and EURIBOR 3M as the foreign index, i.e.\ Libor-type foreign coupons), GBPUSD (SONIA swaps and SONIA vs SOFR resetting CB swaps) and USDJPY (TONA swaps and TONA vs SOFR resetting CB swaps, FX quoted inverted). They are described in \autoref{rema:cal_example_markets}. Their quotes are rounded and illustrative. They are not live market data. Unless stated otherwise, ``the example market'' below is EURUSD.


\subsection{Calibration instruments}\label{ssec:calibration_instruments}

We write $\kappa^e = 1-\theta S^{e|e,\div} = 1+\theta s^e$ for the constant multiplicative funding spread of the index of currency $e$ with tenor $\theta$ \eqref{eq:constant_funding_spread}, $\kappa^e = 1$ for an OIS index, whose coupons are compounded overnight (\autoref{rema:rfr_no_funding_spread}). For a compounded coupon, $L$ denotes the forward coupon rate \eqref{eq:forward_coupon}. The spot date is $0$.

\begin{defi}[Calibration instruments]\label{defi:calibration_instruments}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Swaps.} A swap in currency $e$ under native collateral on $[T_0,T_n]$ has floating dates $T_0 = t^f_0 < \ldots < t^f_m = T_n$ every index tenor $\theta$ and fixed dates $t^x_1 < \ldots < t^x_p = T_n$ with accruals $\tau_k$ (its own frequency). With $Z_t = Z^{e|e}_{0,t}$, its forward par rate is
\begin{eqnarray}
  Y ~=~ \frac{\Fl}{A}, \qquad \Fl ~=~ \sum_{i=1}^m \left( \kappa^e Z_{t^f_{i-1}} - Z_{t^f_i} \right) ~=~ \sum_{m'} c^f_{m'} Z_{t^f_{m'}} , \qquad A ~=~ \sum_{k=1}^p \tau_k Z_{t^x_k} ,
  \label{eq:cal_swap_rate}
\end{eqnarray}
since $1+\theta L^{e|e}_{0,t^f_{i-1};\theta} = \kappa^e Z_{t^f_{i-1}}/Z_{t^f_i}$. The coefficients are $c^f = \kappa^e$ at $T_0$, $\kappa^e - 1$ at the intermediate floating dates and $-1$ at $T_n$. For an OIS (RFR) swap, $\kappa^e = 1$ holds exactly by \autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward}, whatever the floating frequency and the compounding (daily or continuous): the floating leg is $Z_{T_0} - Z_{T_n}$. The GBP and JPY swaps of the examples are annual fixed against compounded SONIA or TONA.
\item \emph{Rate futures.} A future on the index of currency $e$ over $[a,b]$ (e.g.\ $3$M SOFR) is quoted as the price $100(1-R^{\mathrm{fut}})$, with
\begin{eqnarray}
  R^{\mathrm{fut}} ~=~ L^{e|e}_{0,a;b-a} + \gamma_{a,b} , \qquad 1+(b-a)L^{e|e}_{0,a;b-a} ~=~ \kappa^e\,\frac{Z^{e|e}_{0,a}}{Z^{e|e}_{0,b}} ,
  \label{eq:cal_future}
\end{eqnarray}
where the forward is model-free (\autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward} for compounded coupons) and $\gamma_{a,b} = \mathrm{Fut}^e_{0,a;b-a} - L^{e|e}_{0,a;b-a}$ is the futures convexity adjustment of \eqref{eq:future}. It depends on the model; as in market practice it is \emph{marked} (an input of the quote, see the footnote of the introduction of this section), not computed from the LGCM. The example SOFR strip uses the Ho--Lee value $\gamma_{a,b} = \frac12\sigma^2ab$ with $\sigma = 95$bp ($0$ to $1.58$bp over $0$--$2$Y).
\item \emph{FX forwards.} $d$-collateralised, quoted as the outright $x^{df|d}_{0,T}$ or as points $(x^{df|d}_{0,T} - x^{df}_0)\cdot\pi$, with the pip factor $\pi = 10^4$. In the \emph{inverted} quotation the market quotes the reciprocal pair (units of $f$ per unit of $d$, e.g.\ USDJPY in JPY per USD when $d = $ USD and $f = $ JPY): the spot quote is $1/x^{df}_0$, the outright is $1/x^{df|d}_{0,T}$ and the points are $(1/x^{df|d}_{0,T} - 1/x^{df}_0)\cdot\pi$ ($\pi = 100$ for USDJPY). The model state is always $x = x^{df}$, so the model spot is the reciprocal of the quoted spot. This is exact: $1/x^{df|d}_{0,T} = x^{fd}_0Z^{d|d}_{0,T}/Z^{f|d}_{0,T}$ is the $d$-collateralised forward of the inverted pair.
\item \emph{Resetting CB swaps.} $d$-collateralised, on the schedule $T_j = T_0 + j\theta$, $j = 0,\ldots,n$, with $\theta$ the tenor of the foreign index. With $P_j = Z^{f|d}_{0,T_j}$ and $F_j = Z^{f|f}_{0,T_j}$, the par spread is
\begin{eqnarray}
  B ~=~ \frac{\sum_{j=1}^n \theta P_j B_j}{\sum_{j=1}^n \theta P_j} , \qquad
  \theta B_j ~=~ \kappa^d \frac{P_{j-1}}{P_j} - \kappa^f \frac{F_{j-1}}{F_j} K_j ,
  \label{eq:cal_ccs_spread}
\end{eqnarray}
where $\theta B_j = (1+\theta L^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}) - (1+\theta L^{f|d}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta})$ is the swaplet spread of \eqref{eq:forward_cb_swaplet_resetting} and
\begin{eqnarray}
  K_j ~=~ \frac{1+\theta L^{f|d}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}}{1+\theta L^{f|f}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}} ~=~ K^C_0(T_{j-1},T_j)
\end{eqnarray}
is the collateral convexity of the foreign coupon: the Libor collateral convexity for Libor coupons, the compounded one for RFR coupons. This is \autoref{prop:rfr_ccs_swaplet} at $t = 0$: the formula holds for both coupon types, the domestic (resetting) leg is model-free, and the coupon type only changes $\kappa^d$, $\kappa^f$ and $K_j$.
\item \emph{Swaptions.} An ATM option on a native forward-starting swap with expiry $T_0$ (strike $K = Y$), quoted as the normal volatility $\sigma_N$ defined from the payer price $\mathrm{PV}$ by $\mathrm{PV} = A_0\,\sigma_N\sqrt{T_0/(2\pi)}$, $A_0 = \sum_k\tau_k Z^{e|e}_{0,t^x_k}$ (ATM Bachelier price under the annuity measure).
\item \emph{CB swaptions.} An ATM option on the forward resetting CB spread $B_{T_0} = B^{f/d|d,r}_{T_0,T_0;M}$, quoted as the normal volatility $\sigma_N = \EE^{f|d,A}[(B_{T_0}-B_0)^+]\sqrt{2\pi/T_0}$ under the annuity measure.
\item \emph{FX options.} An ATM option on $x^{df}_T$, quoted as a Black volatility $\sigma_\BS$. In the LGCM, $\ln x^{df}_T$ is Gaussian under $\QQ^{d|d,T}$ with variance $w^{df}_0(T) = \int_0^T|\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}|^2du$, which does not depend on the collateral, so the quote is $\sigma_\BS^2 T = w^{df}_0(T)$. In the inverted quotation the option is on $1/x^{df}_T$; its ATM Black volatility is the same (\autoref{prop:cal_fx_inverted}), so the quote is used as is.
\end{enumerate}
\end{defi}

\begin{prop}[FX options under the inverted quotation]\label{prop:cal_fx_inverted}
Let $x = x^{df}$ and $\tilde{x} = 1/x = x^{fd}$, and $F = x^{df|d}_{0,T}$. Consider a call on $\tilde{x}_T$ with strike $\tilde{K}$ paid in $f$ and a put on $x_T$ with strike $K = 1/\tilde{K}$ paid in $d$, both $d$-collateralised, and their Black volatilities, defined with the forwards $\tilde{F} = \EE^{f|d,T}[\tilde{x}_T]$ and $F = \EE^{d|d,T}[x_T]$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item In any arbitrage free model, $\tilde{F} = 1/F$ and the two Black volatilities are equal. In particular the ATM Black volatility of $1/x$ (strike $1/F$) equals the ATM Black volatility of $x$ (strike $F$), and the same holds for calls and puts by put-call parity.
\item In the LGCM, both smiles are flat: $\sigma_\BS^2T = w^{df}_0(T)$ at every strike.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
1. By the change of currency formula for FX forwards in the section on collateral convexity, $\EE^{d|d,T}[x_Tv^f_T] = F\,\EE^{f|d,T}[v^f_T]$ for any payoff $v^f_T$ in $f$. With $v^f = \tilde{x}_T$ this gives $\tilde{F} = 1/F$, and with $v^f = (\tilde{x}_T-\tilde{K})^+$,
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \EE^{f|d,T}\left[(\tilde{x}_T-\tilde{K})^+\right] ~=~ \frac{1}{F}\EE^{d|d,T}\left[x_T\left(\tfrac{1}{x_T}-\tilde{K}\right)^+\right] ~=~ \frac{\tilde{K}}{F}\,\EE^{d|d,T}\left[(K-x_T)^+\right] .
\end{eqnarray*}
Let $\sigma$ be the Black volatility of the put, $\EE^{d|d,T}[(K-x_T)^+] = KN(-d_2)-FN(-d_1)$ with $d_{1,2} = (\ln(F/K)\pm\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2T)/(\sigma\sqrt{T})$. The right hand side is then $N(-d_2)/F - \tilde{K}N(-d_1)$. The Black call price of $\tilde{x}$ with forward $1/F$, strike $\tilde{K}$ and the same $\sigma$ is $\frac{1}{F}N(\tilde{d}_1)-\tilde{K}N(\tilde{d}_2)$ with $\tilde{d}_{1,2} = (\ln(1/(F\tilde{K}))\pm\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2T)/(\sigma\sqrt{T}) = -d_{2,1}$, the same number. Black prices are strictly increasing in the volatility, so $\sigma$ is the Black volatility of the call on $\tilde{x}$. ATM, $\tilde{K} = 1/F$ corresponds to $K = F$.

2. $\ln x_T$ is Gaussian under $\QQ^{d|d,T}$ with variance $w^{df}_0(T)$ (\autoref{prop:cal_constraints}, item 3), so the put prices are Black prices with $\sigma^2T = w^{df}_0(T)$ at every strike, and item 1 transfers this to $\tilde{x}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}
Item 1 is the usual symmetry of FX volatilities: it uses only the FX forward identity, not the model. The implementation (\texttt{FXForward} with \texttt{inverted=True} and \texttt{MarketQuotes.}\allowbreak\texttt{fx\_inverted} in \texttt{instruments.py}) converts the inverted spot and forwards to the model pair and leaves the FX volatility quotes unchanged. The test \texttt{test\_fx\_option\_vol\_is\_}\allowbreak\texttt{invariant\_under\_inversion} checks the price identity of item 1 by adaptive quadrature of the lognormal density. In the Monte Carlo check the standard error of an inverted quote $g(F)$ is propagated by the delta method, $\pi s_F/F^2$ for points with $s_F$ the standard error of the outright $F$ (the second order bias, $O(s_F^2/F^3)$, is far below the standard error).
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Curve keys]
A discount curve $Z^{e|c}$ is identified by the pair (currency $e$, collateral overnight index $c$), e.g.\ $Z^{f|d} = $ (EUR, USD SOFR). The calibrated curves are $Z^{d|d}$, $Z^{f|f}$ and $Z^{f|d}$, the three initial curves of \autoref{theo:qgcm}. The off-market curve $Z^{d|f}$ is a model output (see \autoref{ssec:cal_off_market}): it is not bootstrapped from quotes.
\end{rema}


\subsection{Discount curves}

\begin{prop}[Model-free curves]\label{prop:cal_model_free_curves}
Assume constant funding spreads \eqref{eq:constant_funding_spread}. Then, in any arbitrage free model:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the par rates of native swaps are functions of the native discount curve $Z^{e|e}_{0,\cdot}$ only, given by \eqref{eq:cal_swap_rate};
\item the cross-currency curve is determined by the $d$-collateralised FX forwards:
\begin{eqnarray}
  Z^{f|d}_{0,T} &=& \frac{x^{df|d}_{0,T}\, Z^{d|d}_{0,T}}{x^{df}_0} .
\end{eqnarray}
\end{enumerate}
In particular, the native curves and $Z^{f|d}_{0,\cdot}$ up to the last FX forward pillar do not depend on the model. With piecewise flat forwards and one node per pillar, the native curves are obtained by a sequential bootstrap of the swap quotes.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
1. Each floating coupon is replicated by $\kappa^e$ zero coupon bonds maturing at its start minus one at its end, by \eqref{eq:constant_funding_spread} and the definition of $L^{e|e}$ as an FRA par rate for Libor coupons, and by \autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward} (with $\kappa^e = 1$ and no hypothesis) for compounded ones; fixed coupons are zero coupon bonds. 2. This is \eqref{eq:fx_forward} at $t=0$ with $i=d$.
\end{proof}

Beyond the last FX forward, the resetting CB swaps determine $Z^{f|d}$, but \eqref{eq:cal_ccs_spread} also involves the coupon collateral convexities $K_j = K^C_0(T_{j-1},T_j)$. They depend on the model: $K_j = 1$ in the deterministic collateral basis (DCB) model, and in the LGCM they are given by \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} at $t=0$: \eqref{eq:coupon_convexity_daily} for daily compounded coupons and, for Libor coupons, by \eqref{eq:libor_collateral_convexity_lgcm},
\begin{eqnarray}
  K_j &=& \exp\left\{ -\int_0^{T_{j-1}} \Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T_{j-1};\theta}\cdot\Sigma^{f\to d}_{u,T_j}\,du \right\} .
  \label{eq:cal_libor_convexity}
\end{eqnarray}
So the curve bootstrap only needs a \emph{convexity provider} that returns $K_j$.

\begin{algo}[Curve bootstrap with a convexity provider]\label{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap}
Input: linear quotes (swaps, FX forwards, resetting CB swaps) and a pricer that returns $K(T_{j-1},T_j)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Group the quotes by the curve they determine, and process the curves in dependency order: $Z^{d|d}$ and $Z^{f|f}$ first, then $Z^{f|d}$, whose FX forwards read $Z^{d|d}$ and whose CB swaps read $Z^{f|f}$.
\item Native curves have piecewise flat instantaneous forwards. The curve $Z^{f|d}$ is the product of $Z^{f|f}$ and the collateral basis curve $\QZ^{f|f\to d}$, which has piecewise flat forwards (see the remark ``Which curve to interpolate'' in \autoref{ssec:interpolation_consistency}).
\item On each curve, sort the quotes by (priority, pillar), FX forwards before CB swaps. For each quote whose pillar lies beyond the last node, add a node at the pillar. The flat forward on the new segment is the only unknown: solve (model quote) $=$ (market quote) by a bracketed 1D root search (Brent).
\item A quote whose pillar is not beyond the last node of its curve is not used. It is reported as a check.
\item Flat extrapolation after the last pillar.
\end{enumerate}
The DCB market practice is the case $K_j = 1$ (\texttt{calibrate\_curves(quotes)}). The LGCM uses the coupon convexity of the foreign index (\eqref{eq:cal_libor_convexity} for Libor, \eqref{eq:coupon_convexity_daily} for daily compounding with the step of the index, $1/365$ by default) with the current model parameters.
\end{algo}

\begin{rema}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Flat forwards are an interpolation choice, not a consequence of the model. They make each bootstrap step a scalar equation, as in the DCB stripping of \autoref{ssec:dd_curve_stripping}, where the unknown is the average forward rate on the segment.
\item Quotes inside an already built section (e.g.\ a CB swap shorter than the last FX forward) are checks, not targets. On the example market all $31$ linear quotes set a node and there are no checks.
\item In the example, $\rho^{f,c}<0$ and $K_j>1$ (\autoref{rema:cal_collateral_convexity}): the LGCM curve bootstrapped from the same quotes differs from the DCB curve after the last FX forward.
\item Futures are bootstrapped like the other linear quotes, before them on the same curve (priority $-1$): each future sets the flat forward up to its end date. Swaps maturing inside the futures strip then become checks (see \autoref{rema:cal_global_vs_bootstrap}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{rema}

\subsubsection{Global curve calibration}\label{ssec:cal_global_curves}
The bootstrap ties the interpolation to the quotes: one node per quote, one scalar equation per node, and piecewise flat forwards, whose jumps at the pillars are visible in \autoref{fig:cal_curve_forwards}. Current practice separates the two: the curve has its own parametrisation, piecewise flat where the market quotes forward rates directly (futures, FX forwards) and smooth where it quotes long par rates, and all its parameters are fitted together by non-linear least squares. Curves that depend on each other are calibrated as nested curve sets.

\begin{defi}[Hybrid forward curve]\label{defi:cal_hybrid_curve}
Let $0 = s_0 < s_1 < \ldots < s_m$ (flat nodes, $m\ge1$) and $s_m = k_0 < k_1 < \ldots < k_n$ (spline knots, $n\ge0$). The hybrid curve with parameters $x = (f_1,\ldots,f_m,a_1,\ldots,a_n)$ has the instantaneous forward
\begin{eqnarray*}
  r_{0,t} ~=~ f_k \quad (s_{k-1}<t\le s_k), \qquad r_{0,t} ~=~ I'(t) \quad (s_m < t \le k_n), \qquad r_{0,t} ~=~ I'(k_n) \quad (t > k_n),
\end{eqnarray*}
where $I$ is the cubic spline on the knots $k_0,\ldots,k_n$ with $I(k_i) = \sum_{j\le i} a_j(k_j-k_{j-1})$, $I'(k_0) = f_m$ and $I''(k_n) = 0$. So $a_i$ is the average forward on the section $(k_{i-1},k_i]$ and $\int_{s_m}^t r_{0,u}du = I(t)$ on the spline section; $-\ln Z_{0,t}$ is explicit everywhere. The forward is piecewise flat on $(0,s_m]$, continuous at $s_m$, piecewise quadratic and $C^1$ on $[s_m,k_n]$ and flat after $k_n$, with $r'_{0,k_n} = 0$, so it is $C^1$ at $k_n$ as well. A cross-currency curve is $Z^{f|d} = Z^{f|f}\,\QZ^{f|f\to d}$ with a hybrid collateral basis curve $\QZ^{f|f\to d}$ (the remark ``Which curve to interpolate'').
\end{defi}

\begin{prop}[Smooth forward section]\label{prop:cal_smooth_forward}
Among the $C^2$ functions $J$ on $[k_0,k_n]$ with $J(k_i) = I(k_i)$, $i=0,\ldots,n$, and $J'(k_0) = f_m$, the spline $I$ of Definition~\ref{defi:cal_hybrid_curve} minimises $\int_{k_0}^{k_n}(J'')^2 = \int_{k_0}^{k_n} (r'_{0,t})^2dt$: among the forward curves with the same section averages, continuous at the junction, it has the least squared slope.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Let $\phi = J - I$, so $\phi(k_i) = 0$ and $\phi'(k_0) = 0$. Then $\int(J'')^2 = \int(I'')^2 + 2\int I''\phi'' + \int(\phi'')^2$. On $[k_{i-1},k_i]$, $I'''$ is a constant $c_i$, and integrating by parts, $\int_{k_{i-1}}^{k_i} I''\phi'' = [I''\phi']_{k_{i-1}}^{k_i} - c_i[\phi]_{k_{i-1}}^{k_i} = [I''\phi']_{k_{i-1}}^{k_i}$. Summing over $i$ ($I''$ is continuous), $\int I''\phi'' = I''(k_n)\phi'(k_n) - I''(k_0)\phi'(k_0) = 0$ by $I''(k_n) = 0$ and $\phi'(k_0) = 0$. Hence $\int(J'')^2 = \int(I'')^2 + \int(\phi'')^2 \ge \int(I'')^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Why the spline is on the integrated forward]
Swap rates identify discount factors, i.e.\ integrals of the forward, not its point values. \autoref{tab:cal_zigzag} compares a natural cubic spline of the forward through free knot values $r_{0,k_i}$ (flat forward to $1$Y), fitted exactly to the nine USD swaps of the example, with the hybrid curve fitted to the same swaps: the fit is exact in both cases, but the knot-value spline zig-zags (negative control in \path{tests/test_curve_fitting.py}). Parametrising by the section averages $a_i$ keeps the parameters close to what the quotes measure, and \autoref{prop:cal_smooth_forward} says what is smooth about the result.
\begin{table}[h]
\centering\footnotesize
\input{figures/table_zigzag.tex}
\caption{Instantaneous forward at the knots, USD swaps of the EURUSD example (DCB), both fits exact.}
\label{tab:cal_zigzag}
\end{table}
\end{rema}

\begin{algo}[Nested global curve calibration]\label{algo:cal_global_curves}
Input: linear quotes (swaps, futures, FX forwards, resetting CB swaps), a pricer that returns $K(T_{j-1},T_j)$ (as in \autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap}), and for each target curve the nodes of Definition~\ref{defi:cal_hybrid_curve}. By default, the flat nodes are the end dates of the futures and the FX forward maturities (or the first pillar if the curve has neither) and the spline knots are the remaining pillars.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Group the target curves into \emph{nested curve sets} by dependency level: level $0$ are the curves whose quotes read no other target ($Z^{d|d}$, $Z^{f|f}$), level $\ell$ the curves whose quotes read curves of level $<\ell$ ($Z^{f|d}$, level $1$).
\item Level by level, with the curves of lower levels frozen, find the parameters $x$ of all the curves of the level by Levenberg--Marquardt on
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \min_x \sum_q w_q^2\left(\frac{\text{model}_q(x) - \text{market}_q}{u_q}\right)^2 + \lambda\, 10^8 \sum_{\text{curves}}\int (r'_{0,t})^2\,dt ,
\end{eqnarray*}
with $u_q$ = $1$bp for rates and spreads, $0.01$ for futures prices ($1$bp of rate) and $1$ pip for FX forwards, weights $w_q$ ($1$ by default) and a curvature penalty $\lambda\ge0$ ($0$ by default). The penalty is a sum of squares in closed form: $r' = I''$ is linear on each section.
\item Variant: a single global problem with all the target curves (\texttt{nested=False}).
\end{enumerate}
With one quote per parameter and $\lambda = 0$ the problem is square and the fit is exact. With more quotes than parameters (e.g.\ $1$Y and $2$Y swaps inside a $2$Y futures strip) it is a least squares compromise and the residuals are reported. With fewer, a positive $\lambda$ is required. In the LGCM calibration, \autoref{algo:cal_lgcm} uses this algorithm instead of \autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap} in steps 1 and 3 when \texttt{ModelSpec(curves=GlobalFit())}; the fixed point with $\lambda^c$ is unchanged and warm starts each fit from the previous one.
\end{algo}

\begin{rema}[Global fit against the bootstrap]\label{rema:cal_global_vs_bootstrap}
Example markets, deterministic collateral basis (DCB) curves unless stated (tests in \path{tests/test_curve_fitting.py}; figures and table by \path{python -m convexity.figures}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Two independent algorithms, one curve.} With flat nodes at every pillar and no spline knots, the global fit returns the bootstrapped curves of \autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap} (discount factors agree to $10^{-12}$ relative up to $40$Y). Quotes generated from given hybrid curves are fitted back to the same parameters ($10^{-11}$), nested or in a single problem.
\item \emph{Shape.} \autoref{fig:cal_curve_forwards} compares the forwards. Both curves reprice every quote (\autoref{tab:cal_global_fit}); they agree where the quotes pin the discount factors and differ between the pillars and in the extrapolation, flat at $r_{0,k_n}$ for the hybrid curve and at the last section average for the bootstrap.
\item \emph{Futures.} With the SOFR strip, the $1$Y and $2$Y swaps lie inside the futures section. The bootstrap reprices the futures and leaves the two swaps as checks; the least squares spreads the inconsistency over the futures and the swaps (\autoref{fig:cal_curve_futures}~(a)) and satisfies the first order conditions $J^\top r = 0$ (checked with a finite difference Jacobian).
\item \emph{LGCM.} With \texttt{GlobalFit()} the calibration reprices all $65$ quotes of each market, in as many fixed point iterations as with the bootstrap (\autoref{tab:cal_global_fit}). The calibrated volatilities move by at most about $1\%$ (the long $\lambda^d$, $\lambda^f$ pieces, whose swaptions see the forwards between pillars). The collateral convexity of the $30$Y $Z^{f|d}$ zero rate (LGCM minus DCB) is the same with both interpolations to a few hundredths of a bp: it is a property of the model, not of the interpolation (the remark ``Interpolation and convexity adjustments decouple in the LGCM'').
\item \emph{Locality.} The spline is not local (\autoref{fig:cal_curve_futures}~(b)): a bump of one long swap moves the bootstrapped forwards on its section and the next only, but the hybrid forwards on every section. Smoothness is paid for with less local hedge ratios.
\item \emph{Smoothing.} With more parameters than quotes the fit requires a penalty, and increasing it trades fit for smoothness monotonically.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{table}[h]
\centering\footnotesize
\input{figures/table_curve_fit.tex}
\caption{Bootstrap (B) against global fit (G). DCB curves: largest residual of the global fit over all quotes (bp; pips for FX forwards), residual evaluations per nested level, wall time. LGCM calibration: fixed point iterations, wall time, largest relative change of $\lambda^d,\lambda^f,\lambda^c,\sigma^x$ on any piece, collateral convexity of the $30$Y $Z^{f|d}$ zero rate (LGCM minus DCB on the same quotes).}
\label{tab:cal_global_fit}
\end{table}
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/curve_forwards.pdf}
\caption{Instantaneous forwards of the EURUSD example with the SOFR futures (DCB): bootstrap with flat forwards (dashed) against the global fit of hybrid curves (solid); ticks mark the pillars. The collateral basis forward is $r^{f|d}_{0,t} - r^{f|f}_{0,t}$.}
