\subsection{Calibration to single currency markets}

\subsubsection{Calibration of spot-volatility to the cap market}%
Not implemented: the companion calibrates the native blocks to swaptions only.

\subsubsection{Calibration of spot-volatility to the swaption market}%
We consider a native block $a\in\{d,f\}$ and a swaption in currency $a$ under native collateral. For a swap with cash flow dates $t_m$ (the union of the floating and fixed dates), we write $c_m$ for the coefficients of the payer swap at the ATM strike $K$: $c^f_m$ of \eqref{eq:cal_swap_rate} on the floating dates and $-K\tau_k$ on the fixed dates, summed for equal dates. We also write $P_m = Z^{a|a}_{0,t_m}/Z^{a|a}_{0,T_0}$.

\begin{prop}[Exact one factor swaption]\label{prop:cal_exact_swaption}
Assume the block $a$ has one factor, and write $z = z^a_{T_0}$, $y = y^{a,a}_{T_0}$, $G_m = G^a_{T_0,t_m}$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Under $\QQ^{a|a,T_0}$, $z\sim N(0,y)$ and $Z^{a|a}_{T_0,t} = P(t)\exp(-G^a_{T_0,t}z - \frac{1}{2}(G^a_{T_0,t})^2y)$, with $P(t) = Z^{a|a}_{0,T_0,t}$.
\item The swap value at $T_0$ is $V(z) = \sum_m c_mP_m e^{-G_mz-\frac{1}{2}G_m^2y}$. Assume there is $z^*$ such that $V(z)>0$ iff $z>z^*$ (so $V(z^*)=0$). Then the payer swaption price is
\begin{eqnarray}
  \mathrm{PV} &=& Z^{a|a}_{0,T_0}\, \EE^{a|a,T_0}\left[V(z)^+\right] ~=~ Z^{a|a}_{0,T_0} \sum_m c_mP_m\, \mathcal{N}\left( -\frac{z^*+G_my}{\sqrt{y}} \right) ,
  \label{eq:cal_exact_swaption}
\end{eqnarray}
and the quoted volatility is $\sigma_N = \mathrm{PV}/(A_0\sqrt{T_0/(2\pi)})$.
\item The assumption of 2.\ holds when the coefficients $c_mP_m$, ordered by increasing $G_m$, change sign exactly once, from positive to negative. This is the case for OIS swaps ($\kappa^a = 1$) with $K > -1/\tau_p$, $\tau_p$ the last fixed accrual: ordered by date, the coefficients are $1$ at $T_0$ (where $G = 0$), then $-K\tau_k$ at the fixed dates before the end, all of the sign of $-K$, then $-1-K\tau_p<0$ at the end date. For $K\ge0$ the only positive coefficient is the first one; for $-1/\tau_p<K<0$ every coefficient except the last is positive. In both cases there is exactly one sign change.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
1. The reconstruction formula of Definition~\ref{defi:lgcm} gives $Z^{a|a}_{T_0,t} = P(t)\exp(-G^a_{T_0,t}z - \frac{1}{2}(G^a_{T_0,t})^2y)$ with $y$ deterministic. The change of measure from $\QQ^{d,\RN}$ to $\QQ^{a|a,T_0}$ has a deterministic Girsanov kernel (\autoref{prop:lgcm_drifts} and its foreign analogue), so $z$ is Gaussian, say $N(\mu,v)$. The martingale property $\EE^{a|a,T_0}[Z^{a|a}_{T_0,t}] = P(t)$ gives $-G\mu + \frac{1}{2}G^2(v-y) = 0$ for all $G = G^a_{T_0,t}$, $t\ge T_0$, which cover an interval. Hence $\mu = 0$ and $v = y$ (compare \autoref{lemm:zhat_forward_law}).

2. The first equality is the forward measure pricing formula, and $V(z)^+ = V(z)\vun_{z>z^*}$ by assumption. For each term (exponential tilt),
\begin{eqnarray*}
  e^{-Gz-\frac{1}{2}G^2y}\,\frac{e^{-z^2/(2y)}}{\sqrt{2\pi y}} ~=~ \frac{e^{-(z+Gy)^2/(2y)}}{\sqrt{2\pi y}} ,
\end{eqnarray*}
the density of $N(-Gy,y)$. So $\EE[e^{-Gz-\frac{1}{2}G^2y}\vun_{z>z^*}] = \PP(N(-Gy,y)>z^*) = \mathcal{N}(-(z^*+Gy)/\sqrt{y})$.

3. $G^a_{T_0,t} = \int_{T_0}^tM^a_{T_0,u}du$ is increasing in $t$. Let $G^*$ lie strictly between the largest $G_m$ with a positive coefficient and the smallest $G_m$ with a negative one. Then
\begin{eqnarray*}
  e^{G^*z}V(z) ~=~ \sum_{c_m>0} c_mP_me^{-\frac{1}{2}G_m^2y}\,e^{(G^*-G_m)z} - \sum_{c_m<0}|c_m|P_me^{-\frac{1}{2}G_m^2y}\,e^{(G^*-G_m)z}
\end{eqnarray*}
is strictly increasing (the first sum increases since $G_m<G^*$, the second decreases since $G_m>G^*$), and tends to $-\infty$ as $z\to-\infty$ and to $+\infty$ as $z\to+\infty$. It has a unique zero $z^*$, and $V$ has the sign of $e^{G^*z}V$. For an OIS swap the floating coefficients $c^f_m$ of \eqref{eq:cal_swap_rate} are $1$ at $T_0$, $0$ at the intermediate floating dates ($\kappa^a - 1 = 0$) and $-1$ at the end date, whatever the floating schedule. So the coefficients are $1$ at $T_0$ and $-K\tau_k$, $-1-K\tau_p$ at the fixed dates, and the sign pattern of item 3 follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Libor swaps and what the code checks]
For a Libor swap ($\kappa^a>1$) whose floating leg is more frequent than its fixed leg, the intermediate floating dates carry $\kappa^a-1>0$, so the coefficients change sign many times ($19$ times for the EURIBOR 3M swaps of the example) and item 3 does not apply. The implementation (\texttt{pricers.native\_swaption\_exact\_vol}) only checks $V(-12\sqrt{y})<0<V(12\sqrt{y})$, finds $z^*$ by Brent's method on this bracket and applies \eqref{eq:cal_exact_swaption}. It raises an error if there is no sign change on the bracket. It does not check that the root is unique. On the calibrated example market (16 swaptions $1$Y to $20$Y $\times 10$Y in USD and EUR) we checked numerically that $V$ changes sign exactly once on a grid of $20001$ points of $[-12\sqrt{y},12\sqrt{y}]$, and that \eqref{eq:cal_exact_swaption} agrees with an adaptive quadrature of $\EE[V^+]$ to $2\cdot10^{-9}$ in relative terms for EUR $15$Y$\times10$Y (the quadrature tolerance) and to $7\cdot10^{-14}$ for the others. This is a numerical observation for this market, not a proof for Libor swaps in general.
\end{rema}

\begin{prop}[Gaussian swaption variance with separate fixed and floating schedules]\label{prop:cal_gauss_swaption}
Let the block $a$ have $N_a\ge 1$ factors and use the notations of \eqref{eq:cal_swap_rate} with $P_t = Z^{a|a}_{0,T_0,t}$, $A = \sum_k\tau_kP_{t^x_k}$ and $Y = \sum_m c^f_mP_{t^f_m}/A$. Define the loadings
\begin{eqnarray}
  q_i &=& \frac{1}{A}\left( -\sum_m c^f_m G^{a,i}_{T_0,t^f_m} P_{t^f_m} + Y \sum_k \tau_k G^{a,i}_{T_0,t^x_k} P_{t^x_k} \right) , \qquad i = 1,\ldots,N_a .
\end{eqnarray}
Freezing the gradient of the swap rate with respect to $z^a$ at its forward value, as in \autoref{prop:gauss_swap_rate}, the swap rate at $T_0$ is approximately normal under the annuity measure with mean $Y$ and variance $q^\top y^{a,a}_{T_0}q$. For equal fixed and floating schedules it is the formula of \autoref{rema:gauss_native}.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
By the reconstruction formula, $\partial_{z_i}Z^{a|a}_{T_0,t}|_{z=0} = -G^{a,i}_{T_0,t}Z^{a|a}_{T_0,t}$ at first order in the volatility. So $\partial_{z_i}\Fl = -\sum_mc^f_mG^{a,i}_{T_0,t^f_m}P_{t^f_m}$, $\partial_{z_i}A = -\sum_k\tau_kG^{a,i}_{T_0,t^x_k}P_{t^x_k}$ and $\partial_{z_i}Y = (\partial_{z_i}\Fl - Y\partial_{z_i}A)/A = q_i$. The covariance of $z^a_{T_0}$ is $y^{a,a}_{T_0}$ (\autoref{prop:lgcm_joint_law}). With equal schedules, $c^f_m = \kappa^a\vun_{m=0} + (\kappa^a-1)\vun_{0<m<n} - \vun_{m=n}$ and $\tau = \theta$; regrouping the terms by period gives the loadings of \autoref{rema:gauss_native}.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Accuracy of the Gaussian approximation]\label{rema:cal_gauss_swaption_accuracy}
\autoref{prop:cal_gauss_swaption} is an asymptotic approximation in the volatility. We measured the relative error of the approximate normal volatility against \autoref{prop:cal_exact_swaption} on a $5$Y$\times10$Y EURIBOR 3M swaption (test model of \texttt{tests/test\_calibration.py}) with all volatilities scaled by $\lambda = 0.5, 1, 2$: $2.175\cdot10^{-4}$, $8.702\cdot10^{-4}$, $3.482\cdot10^{-3}$. The ratios are $4.0002$ and $4.0010$, so the error is $O(\lambda^2)$ in this example (numerical observation, not a proof). On the calibrated example market, the approximate volatility exceeds the exact one by $+2.9\cdot10^{-4}$ (EUR $1$Y$\times10$Y) to $+5.4\cdot10^{-3}$ (USD $20$Y$\times10$Y), i.e.\ up to $0.46$bp. The equality with \autoref{rema:gauss_native} for equal schedules was checked to $10^{-12}$ against the independent code of \texttt{gaussian\_approx.approx\_native}, and the two factor variance against loadings and covariances computed by quadrature to $10^{-8}$.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Bootstrap of $\lambda^a$ and initial guess]\label{rema:cal_swaption_bootstrap}
Let $T_1<\ldots<T_n$ be the expiries of the swaptions of block $a$ (one swaption per expiry). The $k$-th swaption sets $\lambda^a$ on $[T_{k-1},T_k)$ ($T_0 = 0$), the last one on $[T_{n-1},\infty)$. A swaption with expiry $T_k$ only reads $\sigma^a$ on $[0,T_k]$, so the bootstrap is triangular: the earlier swaptions are not moved by the later ones. On the segment, $y^{a,a}_{T_k}$ is the integral of $\sigma^{a,i}\sigma^{a,j}$ over $[0,T_k]$ and the loadings $q$ do not depend on the volatilities, so the Gaussian variance is exactly
\begin{eqnarray}
  v(\lambda) &=& v_0 + \lambda^2 v_1 , \qquad v_0 = v(0), \quad v_1 = v(1) - v(0) ,
\end{eqnarray}
and $\lambda = \sqrt{(\sigma_N^2T_k - v_0)/v_1}$ matches the Gaussian approximation in closed form. With one factor, this is the initial guess of a Brent search on the exact volatility of \autoref{prop:cal_exact_swaption}. With several factors, it is the calibrated value: the Gaussian approximation is then the pricer, and the calibration diagnostics flag it. If $v_0\ge\sigma_N^2T_k$, the earlier segments already deliver the target variance (variance squeeze) and the calibration fails. Note that this test uses the Gaussian variance.
\end{rema}

\subsubsection{Calibration of mean-reversion to ATM swaptions}%
Not implemented: mean reversions are marked. The example uses $\beta^d = 3\%$, $\beta^f = 2\%$ and $\beta^c = 10\%$.

\subsubsection{Calibration of mean-reversion to swap rates correlations}%
Not implemented: the correlations and the factor weights are marked.


\newpage%
\subsection{Calibration to the cross-currency market}


\subsubsection{Necessity for joint calibration}%
Let us consider things from the curve building point of view. On the cross-currency market, the most liquid long-dated linear instruments are resetting currency basis swaps. Say we calibrated the model up to tenor $M$, i.e. up to date $T_{\f M}$ where $f = 4$ is the frequency of the swaps.  We now consider the calibration to the tenor $N$. We have
\begin{enumerate}
\item one new target: the currency basis spread $B^{x/u|u,r}_{t_0,T;N}$
\item multiple new unknowns: for each $j=\f M+1,\ldots,\f N$
\begin{enumerate}
\item the discount factors $Z^{x|u}_{t_0,T_j}$
\item the foreign forward rates $L^{x|u}_{t_0,T_{j-1};\theta}$
\item the resetting forward rates $L^{x/u|u}_{t_0,T_{j-1};\theta}$
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
The problem is clearly under-determined.

However, the model provides convexity adjustments that relate the different forward rates to the ones from native single currency markets. But these convexity adjustments depend on the spot volatilities\footnote{as well as the cross-asset correlations. For now, we assume that they are marked but the same approach could be extended to calibrate instantaneous correlations by adding some terminal correlations/covariances as targets.}. Obviously these should be calibrated to match implied volatilities of option prices (FX options and currency basis swaptions) or historical estimations of volatility.

If we assume that correlations are marked by traders, and MR are marked or calibrated externally, we are left with 3 types of unknown parameters:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the cross-currency discount curve $Z^{f|d}_{t_0,T}$
\item the collateral basis spot volatilities  $\sigma^{f\to d}_{t,T}$
\item the FX spot volatilities $\sigma^{df}_{t}$
\end{enumerate}
And since they interact through convexity adjustments, they have to be jointly calibrated.

Moreover, we want the calibration to be local: for risk management purposes we do not want a 2Y FX option to be exposed to 10Y FX implied vols. This implies that we need to bootstrap the parameters.


\subsubsection{Bootstrapping}%

\paragraph{Introduction}
Convexity adjustments induce a lot of interdependence between market quotes. For risk management, we still need to be able to localise the risk. This means that the value of a 1Y FX option for example, should not depend on any market quote with expiry/maturity beyond 1Y.

That is not the case in a global calibration.

The natural way to achieve that is to "bootstrap" all the curves and term structure of parameters.

This can be seen as breaking down the global calibration into steps by sorting calibration targets by expiry/maturity and calibrating model parameters iteratively.

\paragraph{Intuition}
Let us assume we observe spot starting currency basis spreads for a given set of maturities $M_i$.

Let us assume we calibrated the model up to expiry $M_i$.

Then, we can compute the forward starting spread:
\begin{eqnarray}
  A^{f|d}_{0,T_0,T_{\f N}} B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f N}}
  &=& A^{f|d}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}} B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}}  + A^{f|d}_{0,T_{\f M},T_{\f N}} B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_{\f M},T_{\f N}} \\
  B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f N}}  \sum_{j=1}^{\f N} \theta Z^{f|d}_{0,T_{j}}
  &=& A^{f|d}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}} B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}}  + \left( \sum_{j=\f M+1}^{\f N} \theta Z^{f|d}_{0,T_{j}} \right) B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_{\f M},T_{\f N}} \\
  A^{f|d}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}} \left(B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f N}} - B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f M}} \right)
  &=&  \sum_{j=\f M+1}^{\f N} \theta Z^{f|d}_{0,T_{j}}  \left( B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_{j-1},T_{j}} -B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_0,T_{\f N}} \right)
\end{eqnarray}
Unknowns are
\begin{itemize}
\item Currency basis swaplet spreads $B^{f/d|d,r}_{0,T_j;\theta}$
\item Discount rates $R^{f|d}_{0,T_{j};\theta}$
\end{itemize}

The model formulas relate these to the model parameters of which
\begin{itemize}
\item Collateral basis spot vol $\sigma^{f\to d}_{t}$, $t\in [T_{\f M},T_{\f N}[$,
\item FX spot vol\footnote{Actually it does not appear under constant funding spreads.} $\sigma^{df}_{t}$, $t\in [T_{\f M},T_{\f N}[$,
\end{itemize}
which we assume to be constant on the segment $[T_{\f M},T_{\f N}[$, are unknown.

Let us choose a parametrisation for the discount curve $Z^{f|d}$ or at least the collateral spread $\QZ^{f|f\to d}$. For example, we can consider
\begin{itemize}
\item piecewise constant forwards
\item quadratic forwards together with continuity and derivability constraint at $T_{\f M}$
\end{itemize}
The point is that once the interpolation scheme has been chosen, the value of the discount curve on the segment is entirely specified by the average level of rates on the segment:
\begin{eqnarray}
  \overline{r}^{f|d}_{0,T_{\f M},T_{\f N}} &=& \frac{1}{T_{\f N}-T_{\f M}} \int_{T_{\f M}}^{T_{\f N}} r^{f|d}_{0,u} \, du
\end{eqnarray}
So we have reduced the determination of the discount factors $Z^{f|d}_{t_0,T_i}$ to that of a single parameter.

The problem is now well posed since we have 3 unknowns and 3 constraints as listed in \autoref{table:bootstrapping}.
\begin{center}
\begin{table}[h!]
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Unknowns & Constraints \\
\hline
Average discount rate & Currency basis spread \\
ColBasis spot vol & Currency basis spread vol    \\
%IRF-ColB spot corr & IRF-CB terminal correlation   \\
\hline
FX spot vol & FX implied vol \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\medskip
\caption{Bootstrapping\label{table:bootstrapping}}
\end{table}
\end{center}

Note that, while mathematically complex, the correspondence between model parameters and calibration targets is very intuitive. The constraints are made explicit in \autoref{prop:cal_constraints} and the algorithm is \autoref{algo:cal_lgcm}. The pillars of the three constraints need not coincide: in the implementation the collateral basis curve has one node per FX forward and CB swap maturity, $\lambda^c$ one level per CB volatility expiry and $\sigma^x$ one level per FX option expiry.


\subsubsection{Model usage and calibration targets}

\begin{rema}[Model usage and calibration targets]
The bootstrapping algorithm works as long as we have one target per expiry\footnote{With CB spread volatilities of tenor longer than the spacing of the expiries, the volatility of expiry $T_0$ reads $\sigma^c$ beyond $T_0$ (through the coupon convexities $K_{T_0,j}$), so the expiry by expiry bootstrap is no longer triangular. The fixed point of \autoref{algo:cal_lgcm} resolves this dependency.}. There is still a range of choices for the calibration targets that is up to discussion. The answer should be decided based on the model usage.

The model can be used in two distinct ways:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Valuation and risk management. One should specify a set of hedging instruments and calibrate the model these. When an option market is available and these are included in hedging instruments, the internal parameters should be calibrated to options prices.  When no option market is available as is the case for currency basis volatility risk, the covariance parameters should be calibrated to the historical covariances of the corresponding market quotes, i.e. currency basis spreads in this case. Ideally one would have only one set of parameters so that pricings are consistent across products and break-even levels are well defined.
\item Benchmarking and reserve usage. One should mark covariance parameters that cannot be implied from market prices conservatively. In particular, these marks should depend on the structure of the portfolio.
\end{enumerate}

Note that since CB delta is going to be hedged with spot-starting resetting cross-currency swaps, one can argue that we should calibrate to their vol. At the same time, it is trivial to synthesise a forward-starting swap.
\end{rema}


\begin{rema}[FX options market]%
The market ignores collateral convexity for FX options. It seems natural to assume FX forwards and volatilities correspond to USD collateralized products.

Note that in the Linear Gaussian model, the volatility of the FX process is invariant under collateral but the FX forward is not. This means that the calibration targets we should chose may not be the standard ATM options. We may want to calibrate to the volatilities corresponding to the adjusted FX forward. In the implementation, the ATM strike is the $d$-collateralised forward.

Assuming all other parameters are set and we chose target implied volatilities, the FX spot volatility $\sigma^{df}_\cdot$ can be bootstrapped in closed form to match ATM volatilities (\autoref{prop:cal_constraints}). Calibration can only fail if we encounter a variance squeeze.
\end{rema}


\subsection{Bootstrapping algorithm}%

\subsubsection{Calibration constraints}%

We use the notations of Definition~\ref{defi:calibration_instruments}, and $\Sigma^c = \Sigma^{f\to d}$, so that $\Sigma^{d\to f} = -\Sigma^c$.

\begin{prop}[Calibration constraints]\label{prop:cal_constraints}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{CB spread.} Write $P_j = F_j\,\QZ_j$ with $\QZ_j = \QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,T_j}$. On a bootstrap segment, $\QZ$ has a flat forward $\bar{q}$ after the last node, and the model CB spread is \eqref{eq:cal_ccs_spread} with $K_j = K^C_0(T_{j-1},T_j)$ given by \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} (\eqref{eq:cal_libor_convexity} for Libor coupons):
\begin{eqnarray}
  B(\bar{q}) &=& \frac{1}{\theta\sum_{j}P_j}\sum_{j=1}^n F_j\QZ_j \left( \kappa^d \frac{F_{j-1}\QZ_{j-1}}{F_j\QZ_j} - \kappa^f \frac{F_{j-1}}{F_j} K_j \right) .
\end{eqnarray}
It depends on the collateral basis volatility only through $K_j$, which reads $\sigma^c$ and $\sigma^f$ on $[0,T_{n-1}]$ for Libor coupons and up to the last overnight fixing before $T_n$ for daily compounded ones (the in-arrears term of \autoref{prop:coupon_continuous_error}). The DCB model is the case $K_j = 1$.
\item \emph{CB spread volatility.} The model volatility of the forward CB spread of expiry $T_0$ is given by the exact distribution of $B_{T_0}$ under $\QQ^{f|d,A}$ (\autoref{lemm:zhat_forward_law} and \autoref{lemm:par_spread_state_functions}, 2D quadrature) and, approximately, by \autoref{prop:gauss_cb_spread}: $\sigma_N^2T_0\approx\hat{q}_B^\top\hat{y}_{T_0}\hat{q}_B$. The exact value uses the coupon convexities seen from $T_0$, $K_{T_0,j} = K^C_{T_0}(T_{j-1},T_j)$ (\autoref{rema:gauss_compounded}), e.g.\ $K_{T_0,j} = \exp\{-\int_{T_0}^{T_{j-1}}\Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T_{j-1};\theta}\cdot\Sigma^{c}_{u,T_j}du\}$ for Libor coupons. So it reads $\sigma^c$ on $[0,T_{n-1}]$ for Libor coupons (up to the last overnight fixing before $T_n$ for daily compounded ones), beyond the expiry, and the curve $Z^{f|d}$ on $[T_0,T_n]$.
\item \emph{FX volatility.} The total variance of an FX option of expiry $T$ is
\begin{eqnarray}
  w(T) ~=~ \sigma_\BS^2\, T ~=~ \int_0^T\left|\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\right|^2du , \qquad
  \Sigma^{df}_{u,T} ~=~ \Sigma^{d|d}_{u,T} - \Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T} - \Sigma^{c}_{u,T} + \sigma^{df}_u .
\end{eqnarray}
If $\sigma^x = s$ on the last segment $[t_0,T]$ and all other parameters are fixed, then $w(T) = as^2 + bs + c$ with
\begin{eqnarray*}
  a &=& T - t_0 ~>~ 0 , \\
  b &=& 2\sum_{(e,i)}\epsilon_e\int_{t_0}^T\rho^{(e,i),x}_u\sigma^{e,i}_uG^{e,i}_{u,T}\,du , \qquad \epsilon_d = 1,\ \epsilon_f = \epsilon_c = -1, \\
  c &=& \int_0^{t_0}\left|\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\right|^2du \\
  & & + \int_{t_0}^T\left|\Sigma^{d|d}_{u,T} - \Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T} - \Sigma^c_{u,T}\right|^2du ,
\end{eqnarray*}
all covariance integrals (\autoref{ssec:covariance_integrals}). The FX volatility is matched by a non-negative $s$ iff
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \min_{s\ge0}\left(as^2+bs+c\right) ~\le~ \sigma_\BS^2T ,
\end{eqnarray*}
i.e.\ iff $c\le\sigma_\BS^2T$, or $b<0$ and $c - b^2/(4a)\le\sigma_\BS^2T$. Otherwise there is a variance squeeze. The largest root is
\begin{eqnarray}
  s &=& \frac{-b + \sqrt{b^2 - 4a\left(c - \sigma_\BS^2T\right)}}{2a} .
  \label{eq:cal_fx_root}
\end{eqnarray}
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
1. Substitute $P_j = F_j\QZ_j$ in \eqref{eq:cal_ccs_spread}; $K_j$ is \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} at $t=0$ (\eqref{eq:libor_collateral_convexity_lgcm} for Libor coupons). The daily kernel $\varphi^\delta_u$ vanishes after the last overnight fixing.

2. The CB spread is a function of $\hat{z}_{T_0}$ by \autoref{lemm:par_spread_state_functions}, where the foreign coupon forward under domestic collateral at $T_0$ involves $K_{T_0,j}$ by \autoref{prop:coupon_collateral_convexity} at $t = T_0$, and $\hat{z}_{T_0}$ is Gaussian by \autoref{lemm:zhat_forward_law}.

3. By the proposition on the FX spot dynamic in the LGCM specification,
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\cdot dW_u &=& \sum_{(e,i)}\epsilon_e\sigma^{e,i}_uG^{e,i}_{u,T}\,d\widetilde{W}^{(e,i)}_u + \sigma^x_u\,d\widetilde{W}^x_u .
\end{eqnarray*} On $[0,t_0)$ the integrand does not depend on $s$. On $[t_0,T]$, expand $|\Lambda_u + s\,e_x|^2 = |\Lambda_u|^2 + 2s\,\Lambda_u\cdot e_x + s^2$ with $\Lambda_u = \Sigma^{d|d}_{u,T}-\Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T}-\Sigma^c_{u,T}$ and $\rho^{x,x} = 1$. The values of $\sigma^x$ after $T$ do not enter. Since $a>0$, the minimum of the quadratic over $s\ge 0$ is $c$ if $b\ge 0$ and $c - b^2/(4a)$ if $b<0$; a non-negative root exists iff this minimum is at most the target, and then the largest root \eqref{eq:cal_fx_root} is non-negative.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}
\begin{enumerate}
\item In item 3, $\Sigma^{df} = \Sigma^{d|d} - \Sigma^{f|f} + \Sigma^{d\to f} + \sigma^{df}$, with $\Sigma^{d\to f} = -\Sigma^c$, as in the FX forward dynamic of the reconstruction section. We checked that the total variance of the implementation agrees with explicit loadings $(+G^d,-G^f,-G^c,1)$ to rounding, and that on every FX segment of the calibrated example $w(2) - (4a+2b+c) \le 4\cdot10^{-15}$ (exact quadratic) and $a = T-t_0$ to $10^{-12}$.
\item The implementation computes $a$, $b$, $c$ from $w(0)$, $w(1)$, $w(-1)$ and takes the largest root \eqref{eq:cal_fx_root}. When both roots are non-negative ($c>\sigma_\BS^2T$ and $b<0$) the choice of the largest one is a convention. On the example market $c<\sigma_\BS^2T$ and $b<0$ at every FX expiry, so the non-negative root is unique.
\item The quantities of items 1 and 2 do not involve $\sigma^x$ ($K_j$ and the law of $\hat{z}$ under $\QQ^{f|d,T_0}$ only involve the $f$ and $c$ blocks). So the FX volatilities can be calibrated last, without feedback.
\end{enumerate}
\end{rema}

\subsubsection{Bootstrapping algorithm}%

\begin{algo}[LGCM calibration]\label{algo:cal_lgcm}
Input: the quotes of the introduction, the marked mean reversions, weights, correlations and funding spreads, and a marked level of $\lambda^c$ if there are no CB volatility quotes. The model knots are the union of all option expiries and a horizon ($60$Y).
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Native curves.} Bootstrap $Z^{d|d}$ and $Z^{f|f}$ from the swaps (\autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap}, model-free by \autoref{prop:cal_model_free_curves}).
\item \emph{Rates volatilities.} For $a\in\{d,f\}$, bootstrap $\lambda^a$ expiry by expiry from the swaptions (\autoref{rema:cal_swaption_bootstrap}): Gaussian guess, then the exact price of \autoref{prop:cal_exact_swaption} for one factor.
\item \emph{Fixed point on $(\QZ^{f|f\to d},\lambda^c)$.} Repeat:
\begin{enumerate}
\item bootstrap $Z^{f|d}$ from the FX forwards and the resetting CB swaps (\autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap}) with $K_j$ from the current $\sigma^c$;
\item if CB volatilities are quoted, for each expiry $T_k$ solve for $\lambda^c$ on $[T_{k-1},T_k)$ (the last one on $[T_{n-1},\infty)$): Brent search on the Gaussian approximation of \autoref{prop:gauss_cb_spread}, then Brent search on the exact quadrature started there. Fail if the volatility at $\lambda^c = 0$ on the segment already reaches the target (variance squeeze). This test uses the Gaussian approximation, as for the swaptions (\autoref{rema:cal_swaption_bootstrap});
\end{enumerate}
until the maximum change of the $\lambda^c$ levels and of the forwards of $\QZ^{f|f\to d}$ between two iterations is below $10^{-12}$ (at most $30$ iterations, else fail). Without CB volatilities, one pass with the marked $\sigma^c$.
\item \emph{FX volatility.} For each FX expiry $T_k$, set $\sigma^x$ on $[T_{k-1},T_k)$ (the last one on $[T_{n-1},\infty)$) by \eqref{eq:cal_fx_root}; fail on a variance squeeze.
\end{enumerate}
Output: the model, the curves $Z^{d|d}$, $Z^{f|f}$, $Z^{f|d}$, and the off-market curve $Z^{d|f}$ (\autoref{ssec:cal_off_market}).
\end{algo}

\begin{rema}[Why a fixed point, and why each expiry sets only its own segment]\label{rema:cal_fixed_point}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The curve $Z^{f|d}$ beyond the FX forwards depends on $\sigma^c$ on $[0,30\mathrm{Y}]$ through the $K_j$ (\autoref{prop:cal_constraints}, item 1). The CB volatility of expiry $T_k$ depends on the curve (weights $P_j$, levels $B_j$) and, through $K_{T_k,j}$, on $\sigma^c$ on $[T_k,T_k+\mathrm{tenor}-\theta]$, i.e.\ on the next segment (item 2). Neither ``curve first, then volatilities'' nor ``expiry by expiry'' is triangular, hence the fixed point. The swaption and FX steps are triangular and need no iteration.
\item A level set on $[T_{k-1},\infty)$ instead of $[T_{k-1},T_k)$ would overwrite the pieces read by the earlier CB volatilities. A draft implementation did this: the earlier CB volatilities were then missed by up to $3.8\cdot10^{-11}$. With one segment per expiry and the outer fixed point, they are repriced to $1.5\cdot10^{-17}$.
\item On the example market the fixed point stops after $5$ iterations, with changes $\infty$, $6.2\cdot10^{-4}$, $1.8\cdot10^{-7}$, $4.0\cdot10^{-11}$, $8.2\cdot10^{-15}$: successive ratios $2.8\cdot10^{-4}$, $2.3\cdot10^{-4}$, $2.1\cdot10^{-4}$, a contraction factor of about $2$--$3\cdot10^{-4}$ per iteration. This is a numerical observation; we have no proof of contraction. It is consistent with the size of the coupling: $K_j - 1\le 2.24\cdot10^{-4}$ up to $30$Y. With $\sigma^c = 0$ and no CB volatilities, one pass suffices and the LGCM curve equals the DCB curve to $10^{-14}$ (test \texttt{test\_dcb\_limit}).
\item The whole calibration takes about $6$s on the example market (65 quotes; $5.6$ to $6.3$s measured in different runs on this branch, $6.1$ to $6.8$s for GBPUSD and $6.0$ to $6.6$s for USDJPY with daily coupons, also $5$ fixed point iterations). The calibrated levels are $\lambda^d$ between $1.19\%$ and $1.35\%$, $\lambda^f$ between $0.89\%$ and $1.05\%$, $\lambda^c$ between $9.9$bp and $18.6$bp and $\sigma^x$ between $7.0\%$ and $8.3\%$. Round trip: quotes generated by the calibrated model, calibrated from another initial $\lambda^c$, give back the parameters to $10^{-8}$ (relative) and $Z^{f|d}$ to $10^{-12}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Verification]\label{rema:cal_verification}
All numbers are for the three example markets (\autoref{rema:cal_example_markets}) and the default specification (one factor per block, $\beta^d = 3\%$, $\beta^f = 2\%$, $\beta^c = 10\%$, $\rho^{d,f} = 0.6$, $\rho^{f,c} = -0.4$, $\rho^{d,c} = -0.2$, $\rho^{d,x} = 0.25$, $\rho^{f,x} = 0.3$, $\rho^{c,x} = 0.35$), computed by \texttt{calibration\_check.py}. The GBPUSD and USDJPY markets are calibrated with daily compounded foreign coupons ($\delta = 1/365$).
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Analytic repricing} with the exact pricers (65 quotes per market). Maximum absolute errors, EURUSD / GBPUSD / USDJPY: swaps $2.3\cdot10^{-16}$ / $4.2\cdot10^{-17}$ / $4.2\cdot10^{-17}$, FX forwards $1.45\cdot10^{-12}$ / $1.9\cdot10^{-12}$ / $1.0\cdot10^{-12}$ pips, resetting CB swaps $5.0\cdot10^{-17}$ / $5.2\cdot10^{-17}$ / $1.0\cdot10^{-16}$, swaption normal volatilities $6.4\cdot10^{-17}$ / $1.9\cdot10^{-17}$ / $1.4\cdot10^{-17}$, FX Black volatilities $1.4\cdot10^{-17}$ / $2.8\cdot10^{-17}$ / $4.2\cdot10^{-17}$, CB normal volatilities $1.5\cdot10^{-17}$ / $2.3\cdot10^{-17}$ / $3.6\cdot10^{-17}$. The Gaussian approximations differ from the exact values by $+2.9\cdot10^{-4}$ to $+5.4\cdot10^{-3}$ (relative) for the EURUSD swaptions ($5.4\cdot10^{-3}$ at most in every market), and at most $5.7\cdot10^{-5}$ ($15$Y$\times1$Y) / $7.1\cdot10^{-5}$ / $1.4\cdot10^{-4}$ for CB volatilities.
\item \emph{Monte Carlo repricing} of all 65 quotes with the exact LGCM scheme (quarterly grid refined by the payment and fixing dates), $2^{17}$ antithetic paths, with control variates whose means are exact no-arbitrage identities. For the compounded coupons of GBPUSD and USDJPY, the overnight fixings are streamed through the simulation and the coupon is valued on the paths with a \emph{weekly} step $\delta = 1/52$, both on the paths and in the model (\texttt{mc\_report(..., compounding\_step=1/52)}: $1562$ simulation dates instead of $122$ for EURUSD). The closed form is exact for any step, and the daily to weekly change of the model quotes is at most $0.046$ (GBPUSD) and $0.019$ (USDJPY) standard errors, so the daily calibrated quotes remain the reference. With a \emph{daily} grid on the paths ($11040$ dates, $183$s), the $8$ GBPUSD CB swaps have $|z|\le2.17$ (seed $7$). Largest $|z|$ by type, seed $7$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|c|c|c|}
\hline
 & EURUSD & GBPUSD & USDJPY \\
\hline
swaps & $2.15$ & $1.74$ & $1.76$ \\
FX forwards & $1.62$ & $1.82$ & $1.81$ \\
resetting CB swaps & $2.15$ ($20$Y) & $3.10$ ($30$Y) & $2.70$ ($30$Y) \\
swaptions & $2.22$ & $1.86$ & $1.89$ \\
FX options & $1.84$ & $1.73$ & $1.83$ \\
CB volatilities & $1.53$ & $2.70$ & $2.38$ \\
\hline
all quotes, seed $7$ / $8$ / $9$ & $2.22$ / $2.18$ / $2.71$ & $3.10$ / $2.51$ / $2.89$ & $2.70$ / $2.30$ / $2.65$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All quotes are within $4$ standard errors. Median standard errors (seed $7$, EURUSD / GBPUSD / USDJPY): $0.0039$ / $0.0034$ / $0.0071$bp for CB spreads, $0.11$ / $0.12$ / $0.11$bp for swaption volatilities, $0.024$ / $0.017$ / $0.029$bp for CB volatilities. The CB spread standard errors grow with the maturity, from $0.00076$bp ($2$Y) to $0.0155$bp ($30$Y) for EURUSD. They were halved, compared with an earlier version of the check, by the control variate $C_3 = D^{f|f}_s\prod_{k<n-1}1/Z^{f|f}_{t_k,t_{k+1}}$ (the native rolled account, whose mean $Z^{f|f}_{0,a}$ is the model-free value of \autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward}); for compounded coupons the control $D^{f|d}_s\prod_{k<n-1}1/Z^{f|d}_{t_k,t_{k+1}}$ (mean $Z^{f|d}_{0,a}$ by the same telescoping) was necessary: without it the standard error of a $5$Y GBPUSD CB spread was $0.134$bp instead of $0.0076$bp ($2^{15}$ paths). The option prices are compared as prices: the MC straddle is mapped to a volatility by the same linear map as the pricer, so no Gaussian assumption enters the MC side.
\item \emph{No bias across seeds.} The $z$ of the CB swaps of one run are strongly correlated across maturities (same paths, shared periods), so a run with all $z$ of the same sign is not by itself a sign of bias (e.g.\ seeds $8$ and $9$ of EURUSD, where $7$ of the $8$ CB swaps have $z<0$). Over the $30$ seeds $100$ to $129$ (EURUSD, $2^{17}$ paths), the mean $z$ per maturity lies between $-0.13$ and $+0.26$ with a standard error of the mean of about $0.2$, the mean over all CB swaps is $+0.01\pm0.13$, and the sample standard deviation of $z$ per maturity is between $0.93$ and $1.29$ (about $1\pm0.13$ expected; $1.29$ at $20$Y). A larger sweep (EURUSD, $120$ seeds $100$ to $219$, $2^{13}$ paths, CB swaps $10$, $15$, $20$, $30$Y) gives standard deviations of $z$ of $0.94$, $0.95$, $0.97$ and $1.04$ (each $\pm0.06$), mean $z$ between $-0.04$ and $+0.08$ (standard error of the mean about $0.09$) and $\max|z| = 2.99$ over the $480$ values. So the $1.29$ at $20$Y of the $30$ seed sweep was sampling noise (the largest of $8$ standard deviations, each with standard error about $0.13$), and the standard error of the regression control variate (antithetic pairs averaged first, $p = 3$ controls, in-sample slope with a bias $O(p/N)$) is consistent with the observed spread: no batch-means correction is needed (numerical observation).
\item \emph{Power of the checks.} The CB swap check resolves the collateral convexity of the coupons: the DCB spread ($K_j = 1$) on the calibrated curve is $+35.7$ ($2$Y) to $+205.8$ ($30$Y) standard errors away from the MC value for EURUSD (seed $7$), $+29.5$ to $+202.0$ for GBPUSD and $+12.5$ to $+156.6$ for USDJPY, and $37.9$, $50.0$, $65.8$ standard errors at $5$, $7$, $10$Y in the reduced EURUSD test run ($2^{15}$ paths, seed $11$). With $2^{18}$ paths (seed $3$) the Gaussian swaption approximation is rejected at $7.8$ and $7.0$ standard errors for USD $15$Y$\times10$Y and $20$Y$\times10$Y, while the exact values are at $+0.35$ and $+0.94$. The Gaussian gap of the CB volatilities ($\le1.4\cdot10^{-4}$ relative) is below the MC resolution (about $2.5\cdot10^{-3}$ relative): only the quadrature distinguishes it.
\item \emph{Libor vs compounded coupons are not resolved by the MC.} On the RFR markets, the CB spread computed with the Libor convexity $K^{\mathrm{L}}_j$ in place of the compounded one differs from the model by $+0.00067$bp ($2$Y) to $+0.00099$bp ($30$Y) for GBPUSD and $+0.00059$ to $+0.00109$bp for USDJPY, i.e.\ between $0.05$ and $0.9$ standard errors at $2^{17}$ paths. The MC therefore checks the path valuation of the compounded coupons and rejects $K = 1$, but it cannot tell $K^\delta$ from $K^{\mathrm{L}}$. That distinction rests on the exact benchmark of \autoref{rema:coupon_convexity_verification} and on the deterministic test that the CB spread uses $K^\delta$ (\texttt{test\_rfr\_ccs\_uses\_the\_compounded\_convexity\_not\_libor}: on the test curves of a $20$Y SONIA CB swap with a weekly step, the Libor-$K$ spread differs by $3.7\cdot10^{-7}$, i.e.\ $0.004$bp, and the compounded one is matched to $10^{-13}$ relative).
\item \emph{What is not tested.} The swap and FX forward MC checks are martingale tests of the deflators against the curves ($\EE[D_t] = Z_{0,t}$, $\EE[D_Tx_T] = Z_{0,T}x^{df|d}_{0,T}$). They do not test the calibrated volatilities, and their closed forms are already repriced to $10^{-16}$ analytically. The CB volatility control variate assumes the closed form $K_{T_0,j}$; dropping $K_{T_0,j}-1\le7.2\cdot10^{-7}$ from it is not detected by the MC (it moves the spread by about $0.01$bp, EURUSD).
\end{enumerate}
The tests are in \texttt{tests/test\_calibration.py} and \texttt{tests/test\_ois.py}; the MC tests are marked \texttt{slow}.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Collateral convexity in the calibrated curves]\label{rema:cal_collateral_convexity}
We compare the LGCM calibration with the DCB curve bootstrap (\autoref{algo:cal_curve_bootstrap} with $K_j = 1$) on the same quotes, with the specification of \autoref{rema:cal_verification}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The two $Z^{f|d}$ curves coincide up to the last FX forward ($1$Y), as they must (\autoref{prop:cal_model_free_curves}). After that, the LGCM zero rate of $Z^{f|d}$ is above the DCB one by $+0.17$bp at $5$Y, $+0.63$bp at $10$Y, $+2.05$bp at $20$Y and $+3.75$bp at $30$Y.
\item The Libor collateral convexity of the last period, $K_j - 1$, is $4.4\cdot10^{-5}$ at $10$Y and $2.24\cdot10^{-4}$ at $30$Y. On the calibrated curve, it lowers the $30$Y CB spread by $3.15$bp compared with $K_j = 1$; the DCB curve absorbs this difference in its basis.
\item The FX forward collateral convexity $x^{df|f}_{0,T}/x^{df|d}_{0,T} - 1 = \exp\{\int_0^T\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\cdot\Sigma^c_{u,T}du\} - 1$ is $+3.7$bp at $5$Y, $+15.3$bp at $10$Y and $+119.6$bp at $30$Y. The off-market curve $Z^{d|f}$ has a zero rate $1.53$bp above the DCB off-market curve $Z^{d|d}Z^{f|f}/Z^{f|d}$ of \eqref{eq:usd_off_market_discount_curve} at $10$Y.
\item Sensitivity to $\rho^{f,c}$ (full recalibration, other inputs unchanged): $30$Y shift of the $Z^{f|d}$ zero rate / $10$Y FX forward collateral convexity of $5.63$/$20.95$bp for $\rho^{f,c} = -0.6$, $3.75$/$15.30$bp for $-0.4$, $0.00$/$3.98$bp for $0$ and $-3.76$/$-7.36$bp for $+0.4$. The curve shift vanishes at $\rho^{f,c} = 0$ because, with one factor per block, $K_j$ only involves the $(f,c)$ covariance. The calibrated $\lambda^c(10\mathrm{Y})$ hardly moves ($15.76$ to $15.81$bp). With the other default correlations, the correlation matrix is positive definite only for $\rho^{f,c}$ in about $[-0.7,0.6]$.
\end{enumerate}
The numbers of items 1 to 3 come from a script on the calibrated model; those of item 4 from the notebook \texttt{python/notebooks/lgcm\_calibration.py}.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Example markets]\label{rema:cal_example_markets}
The three example markets of \texttt{market\_data.py} share the USD side: $d = $ USD, collateral and domestic coupons SOFR, SOFR swaps from $4.30\%$ ($1$Y) to $3.80\%$ ($30$Y), USD swaption volatilities $84$ to $110$bp. Each has $65$ quotes: $18$ swaps, $5$ FX forwards ($1$M to $1$Y, points), $8$ resetting CB swaps ($2$Y to $30$Y, quarterly), $16$ swaptions ($1$Y to $20$Y $\times10$Y), $10$ FX options ($1$M to $15$Y) and $8$ CB volatilities ($1$Y to $20$Y $\times1$Y). The quotes are rounded and illustrative, not live data, and they are not smoothed: the forward collateral basis $q$ of the DCB curves (flat per bootstrap segment) jumps between the last FX forward and the first CB swaps. On the segments $0.75$--$1$Y / $1$--$2$Y / $2$--$3$Y it is $+20.4$ / $-9.6$ / $-4.0$bp (EURUSD, a change of sign), $-37.2$ / $-18.7$ / $-34.0$bp (USDJPY) and $-5.2$ / $-2.8$ / $-7.0$bp (GBPUSD). The EURUSD sign flip predates the RFR markets (the EURUSD quotes are those of the original example). These jumps come from the illustrative inputs, not from the model, and the calibrations reprice them.
\begin{enumerate}
\item EURUSD: EUR swaps $2.15\%$ to $2.80\%$ on EURIBOR 3M (funding spread $10$bp, so Libor-type foreign coupons), spot $1.10$ USD per EUR, CB spreads $-8$ to $-14$bp.
\item GBPUSD: SONIA swaps (annual fixed against compounded SONIA) $4.05\%$ ($1$Y) to $3.80\%$ ($30$Y), spot $1.27$ USD per GBP, points $3.1$ ($1$M) to $37.0$ ($1$Y), SONIA vs SOFR CB spreads $-4$ to $-7$bp, GBP swaption volatilities $80$ to $104$bp, FX volatilities $7.8\%$ to $11.5\%$, CB volatilities $6.0$ to $7.5$bp.
\item USDJPY: TONA swaps $0.55\%$ ($1$Y) to $1.80\%$ ($30$Y), spot quoted inverted, $150$ JPY per USD (model spot $x^{df}_0 = 1/150$ USD per JPY), points in $1/100$ JPY from $-49$ ($1$M) to $-593$ ($1$Y), TONA vs SOFR CB spreads $-26$ to $-34$bp, JPY swaption volatilities $45$ to $66$bp, FX volatilities $9.5\%$ to $12.2\%$, CB volatilities $10$ to $13$bp.
\end{enumerate}
The calibration (\autoref{algo:cal_lgcm}) succeeds on the three markets without variance squeeze; every linear quote sets a node ($31$ per market, no checks). Calibrated levels (first piece, peak, last piece): $\lambda^f = 93$, $105$, $89$bp (EURUSD), $105$, $119$, $100$bp (GBPUSD), $50$, $84$, $49$bp (USDJPY); $\lambda^c$ goes from $9.9$ to $18.6$bp (EURUSD), $6.6$ to $12.9$bp (GBPUSD) and $11.0$ to $21.5$bp (USDJPY) between the first and the last piece, mostly increasing with the expiry; $\sigma^x = 7.0$, $8.3$, $7.2\%$ (EURUSD), $7.8$, $9.3$, $8.1\%$ (GBPUSD), $9.5$, $10.5$, $7.6\%$ (USDJPY); $\lambda^d$ is the same in the three markets ($1.19\%$ to $1.35\%$). The LGCM zero rate of $Z^{f|d}$ is above the DCB one by $+0.13$, $+0.50$, $+1.62$, $+2.94$bp at $5$, $10$, $20$, $30$Y for GBPUSD and $+0.13$, $+0.57$, $+1.86$, $+3.19$bp for USDJPY (EURUSD: \autoref{rema:cal_collateral_convexity}, item 1). The largest coupon convexity $K-1$ of a quarterly period up to $30$Y is $2.241\cdot10^{-4}$ (EURUSD, Libor), $1.7607\cdot10^{-4}$ (GBPUSD, daily SONIA) and $1.7314\cdot10^{-4}$ (USDJPY, daily TONA).
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Libor-type vs compounded convexity in the calibrated curves]\label{rema:cal_libor_vs_rfr}
How much does the coupon type matter for the calibrated curves? We recalibrated the RFR markets with the Libor convexity $K^{\mathrm{L}}_j$ in place of the daily compounded one $K^\delta_j$ (foreign index replaced by a Libor-type index with zero funding spread and the same tenor, so the native swaps are unchanged by \autoref{lemm:coupon_native_forward}; every other input is the same). On the calibrated GBPUSD model, $K-1$ of the period ending at $T$ is $1.347954\cdot10^{-6}$ (Libor) against $1.367025\cdot10^{-6}$ (daily) at $T = 2$Y, $3.482846\cdot10^{-5}$ against $3.485167\cdot10^{-5}$ at $10$Y and $1.760386\cdot10^{-4}$ against $1.760651\cdot10^{-4}$ at $30$Y: $\ln(K^\delta/K^{\mathrm{L}}) = 1.9$, $2.3$, $2.6\cdot10^{-8}$, roughly constant as \autoref{prop:coupon_continuous_error} predicts, while $\ln(K^0/K^\delta) = 1.6$ to $2.2\cdot10^{-10}$. The zero rate of the calibrated $Z^{f|d}$ moves by (Libor $K$ minus daily $K$)
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
 & $2$Y & $5$Y & $10$Y & $20$Y & $30$Y \\
\hline
GBPUSD & $-0.00067$bp & $-0.00086$bp & $-0.00091$bp & $-0.00097$bp & $-0.00100$bp \\
USDJPY & $-0.00060$bp & $-0.00097$bp & $-0.00109$bp & $-0.00101$bp & $-0.00096$bp \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
and the calibrated volatilities by at most $7.4\cdot10^{-8}$ (GBPUSD) and $3.9\cdot10^{-8}$ (USDJPY) in relative terms. With the continuous convexity $K^0$ instead of $K^\delta$ the shift is $+0.000005$ to $+0.000009$bp. These are numerical observations on these markets: the in-arrears term of compounded coupons moves the curves by about $0.001$bp, three orders of magnitude below the collateral convexity itself ($+2.9$ to $+3.2$bp at $30$Y against the DCB curve, \autoref{rema:cal_example_markets}), and far below the precision of the quotes. The Monte Carlo cannot resolve it (\autoref{rema:cal_verification}, item 5); the exact benchmark can (\autoref{rema:coupon_convexity_verification}). So, for compounded coupons in these markets, the Libor-type convexity is an excellent approximation; the compounded one is used because it is exact and costs $0.43$ms per period. \autoref{prop:coupon_continuous_error} shows when this could change: the in-arrears term is bounded by $\bar{\sigma}^f\bar{\sigma}^c\theta^3/3$ and does not grow with the expiry, while the Libor term grows with the expiry in these examples (an observation, not a property of the model), so it matters relatively more for long accrual periods (annual coupons) and short expiries ($0.8\%$ of the convexity at $2$Y on the test model of \autoref{rema:coupon_convexity_verification}).
\end{rema}


\subsection{Off-market collateral}\label{ssec:cal_off_market}%

Once the model is calibrated, off-market curves can be computed in closed form using the FX forward convexity adjustment:
\begin{eqnarray*}
  Z^{d|f}_{0,T} ~=~ \frac{x^{df}_0\,Z^{f|f}_{0,T}}{x^{df|f}_{0,T}} , \qquad
  x^{df|f}_{0,T} ~=~ x^{df|d}_{0,T}\exp\left\{\int_0^T\Sigma^{df}_{u,T}\cdot\Sigma^c_{u,T}\,du\right\} .
\end{eqnarray*}
In the DCB model the exponential is $1$ and $Z^{d|f}$ reduces to \eqref{eq:usd_off_market_discount_curve}.


\newpage%
\subsection{Interpolation and consistency with the model dynamic}\label{ssec:interpolation_consistency}

The bootstrapping requires an interpolation scheme for the initial curves on each segment between calibration pillars. We discuss how this choice interacts with the model dynamic. Interpolation enters at two different levels: the initial curves at $t=0$, and the curves at future dates $t>0$.

\subsubsection{Initial curves}

\begin{rema}[Any interpolation is arbitrage free at $t=0$]
In the HJM framework, the initial curves $Z^{d|d}_{0,\cdot}$, $Z^{f|f}_{0,\cdot}$ and $\QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,\cdot}$ are free initial conditions: the drifts are determined by the volatilities alone. So for any absolutely continuous interpolated initial curves (in particular piecewise flat forwards), \autoref{theo:qgcm} defines an arbitrage free model that reproduces them exactly. Contrary to Libor market models (see the remark on LMMs with stochastic currency basis), there is no stub interpolation to make consistent with the dynamic: discount factors at any date are given by the reconstruction formulas. Moreover, the exact simulation only uses the initial curves through discount factors at simulation and payment dates (\autoref{rema:simulation_initial_curves}).
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Interpolation and convexity adjustments decouple in the LGCM]
In the LGCM, the convexity adjustments (e.g.\ $\exp\{-\int\Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T;\theta}\cdot\Sigma^{f\to d}_{u,T+\theta}du\}$) depend on the model parameters only, not on the initial curves. So, on a bootstrapping segment, the collateral basis spot volatility determines the convexity adjustments of all swaplets of the segment, and the interpolation scheme only determines how the average level of the collateral basis is distributed across these swaplets. The model delivered volatility of \autoref{prop:gauss_cb_spread} depends on the initial curves only mildly, through the weights $P_j$ and the levels $L_j$, $B_j$.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Which curve to interpolate]
On a segment between two currency basis pillars, one can either interpolate the cross-currency curve $Z^{f|d}_{0,\cdot}$ or the collateral basis curve $\QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,\cdot} = Z^{f|d}_{0,\cdot}/Z^{f|f}_{0,\cdot}$, the native curve $Z^{f|f}_{0,\cdot}$ being known from the native market. Under the constant funding spread hypothesis and in the LGCM, the swaplet basis spreads are
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \theta B_{j} &=& \kappa^d (1+\theta R^{f|d}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}) - \kappa^f K_j (1+\theta R^{f|f}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}) 
  ~=~ (1+\theta R^{f|f}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}) \left( \kappa^d\, \frac{\QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,T_{j-1}}}{\QZ^{f|f\to d}_{0,T_j}} - \kappa^f K_j \right) 
\end{eqnarray*}
with $\kappa^e = 1-\theta S^{e|e,\div}$ ($1$ for a compounded coupon) and $K_j$ the (deterministic) coupon collateral convexity.
\begin{itemize}
\item With flat forwards for $r^{f|d}_{0,\cdot}$ on the segment, $R^{f|d}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}$ is constant and $B_j$ jumps one for one with the native forwards $R^{f|f}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}$ at the native pillars. The implied collateral basis forward curve $q^{f|f\to d}_{0,\cdot} = r^{f|d}_{0,\cdot} - r^{f|f}_{0,\cdot}$ then carries all the term structure of the native curve with the opposite sign.
\item With flat forwards for $q^{f|f\to d}_{0,\cdot}$ on the segment, $B_j$ only varies through the factor $1+\theta R^{f|f}_{0,T_{j-1};\theta}$ and the convexity $K_j$.
\end{itemize}
Interpolating the collateral basis curve is therefore consistent with the choice of $z^c$ as the state driving $\QZ^{f|f\to d}$ (see the remark on our parametrisation choices in the Quasi-Gaussian section). It also avoids generating a term structure of the short collateral basis $q^{f\to d}_t = q^{f|f\to d}_{0,t} + \vun^\top z^c_t + \vun^\top v^{f,c}_t$, and hence of the FX drift, that only reflects the native curve pillars.
\end{rema}

\subsubsection{Curves at future dates}

\begin{rema}[Calendar-pinned features]
By \autoref{theo:qgcm}, for $t>0$,
\begin{eqnarray*}
  r^{d|d}_{t,T} - r^{d|d}_{0,T} &=& M^{d,\top}_{t,T}\left(z^d_t + y^{d,d}_tG^d_{t,T}\right)
\end{eqnarray*}
and similarly for $r^{f|f}$ and $q^{f|f\to d}$. The right hand side is continuous in $T$, so every discontinuity or kink of the interpolated initial forward curve at a pillar date $T_k$ is still there at the same \emph{calendar} date $T_k$ on every path. On the other hand, a curve re-bootstrapped at $t$ with the same interpolation scheme has its pillars at $t+\tau_k$ for the benchmark tenors $\tau_k$. In general, the two curves differ between the pillars, even if the re-bootstrapping uses the model values of the benchmark quotes.

The practical consequence is that future curves along a simulated path (for exposure, XVA or P\&L explain) should be produced with the reconstruction formulas, not by re-stripping simulated quotes with the curve building code. The latter would price with curves that are not those of the model, which reintroduces exactly the inconsistency the model was designed to remove. For the same reason, the model's carry (roll-down) keeps the initial pillars at fixed calendar dates.
\end{rema}

Whether a parametric interpolation family can be preserved by the dynamic is the consistency problem of Bj\"ork and Christensen \cite{BjorkChristensen}. A family $\mathcal{G}$ of forward curves $\tau\mapsto g(\tau)$, parametrised by time to maturity $\tau = T-t$, is \emph{consistent} with the model if, whenever $\tau\mapsto r_{0,\tau}$ belongs to $\mathcal{G}$, $\tau\mapsto r_{t,t+\tau}$ belongs to $\mathcal{G}$ for all $t$ and all paths.

\begin{prop}[Consistent families for the LGCM]\label{prop:consistent_families}
Assume the parameters of the LGCM are constant and all mean reversions are non zero.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For a native rates block $a$ with mean reversions $\beta^{a,1},\ldots,\beta^{a,N_a}$, the family
\begin{eqnarray*}
  \mathcal{G}^a ~=~ \left\{ \tau\mapsto \zeta_0 + \sum_{i}\zeta_i e^{-\beta^{a,i}\tau} + \sum_{i\le j}\zeta_{ij}e^{-(\beta^{a,i}+\beta^{a,j})\tau} \right\}
\end{eqnarray*}
is consistent with the dynamic of $r^{a}$. If some $\beta^{a,j}$ vanish, the functions $\tau e^{-\beta^{a,i}\tau}$ have to be added.
\item If $\rho^{f,c}\neq 0$, a family of this exponential type is consistent with the dynamic of the collateral basis $q^{f|f\to d}$ only if it contains, in addition to the exponentials of the collateral basis block, the foreign rates exponentials $e^{-\beta^{f,i}\tau}$ and the mixed exponentials $e^{-(\beta^{c,i}+\beta^{f,j})\tau}$ (for generic parameters, i.e. unless their coefficients happen to cancel).
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
With constant parameters, $M^{a,i}_{t,t+\tau} = e^{-\beta^{a,i}\tau}$ and $G^{a,j}_{t,t+\tau} = (1-e^{-\beta^{a,j}\tau})/\beta^{a,j}$ (or $\tau$ if $\beta^{a,j}=0$). By \autoref{theo:qgcm}, $r^a_{t,t+\tau} = r^a_{0,t+\tau} + \sum_i e^{-\beta^{a,i}\tau}(z^{a,i}_t + \sum_j y^{(a,i),(a,j)}_tG^{a,j}_{t,t+\tau})$. The second term lies in the span of $e^{-\beta^{a,i}\tau}$ and $e^{-(\beta^{a,i}+\beta^{a,j})\tau}$. The first term is in $\mathcal{G}^a$ if $r^a_{0,\cdot}$ is, since $e^{-\beta(t+\tau)} = e^{-\beta t}e^{-\beta\tau}$ and constants are shift invariant. For the collateral basis, the reconstruction formula of \autoref{theo:qgcm} contains $M^{f,\top}_{t,T}(v^{f,c}_t + y^{f,c}_tG^c_{t,T})$ and $M^{c,\top}_{t,T}y^{c,f}_tG^f_{t,T}$. When $\rho^{f,c}\neq 0$, $y^{c,f}_t$ and $v^{f,c}_t$ are non zero for $t>0$, and these terms produce $e^{-\beta^{f,i}\tau}$ with coefficient $v^{f,c}_t + y^{f,c}_t(\beta^c)^{-1}\vun$ and $e^{-(\beta^{c,i}+\beta^{f,j})\tau}$ with coefficient $-y^{(c,i),(f,j)}_t\left(1/\beta^{f,j} + 1/\beta^{c,i}\right)$. The exponential decompositions were checked symbolically (sympy).
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}
\begin{enumerate}
\item A consistent family has at most $1+N_a+N_a(N_a+1)/2$ parameters, so it cannot reproduce an arbitrary set of market quotes. There is a genuine trade-off between exact fit of the initial curves (non-parametric interpolation, inconsistent families) and consistency (parametric curves). In the LGCM the exact fit is preferred, and the inconsistency is harmless as long as future curves are produced by the reconstruction formulas.
\item The proposition is in line with \cite{BjorkChristensen}, where the Nelson-Siegel family is shown to be inconsistent with the Hull-White model but can be augmented to achieve consistency. A one factor Hull-White model generates the exponential $e^{-2\beta\tau}$, which is not in the Nelson-Siegel family. See also \cite{Filipovic:NelsonSiegel}: no non-trivial HJM model is consistent with the Nelson-Siegel family.
\item The second part of the proposition says that, if one uses a parametric family for the collateral basis curve, it must include the foreign rates exponentials. The collateral basis curve inherits part of the shape of the foreign curve through the convexity term $\sigma^{f}_{t,T}\cdot\Sigma^{f\to d}_{t,T}$ in its drift.
\end{enumerate}
\end{rema}


\subsection{Alternative calibrations}
For this purpose, we developed the Non Linear Curve Engine: a generic framework to jointly 
\begin{enumerate}
\item calibrate a stochastic spread model 
\item calibrate discount and index curves taking into account the convexity adjustment induced by the model. 
\end{enumerate}

Calibration targets for this joint calibration are 
\begin{enumerate}
\item FX forwards
\item Cross-currency swaps (resetting and non-resetting)
\item FX options
\item Currency basis swaptions (since these are not liquid we cannot imply volatilities so we target historical estimates instead). 
\end{enumerate}

There are several ways to do the calibration.

\subsubsection{Global calibration}%
We create a function with inputs 
\begin{enumerate}
\item curves parameters
\item model parameters
\end{enumerate}
and outputs 
\begin{enumerate}
\item model induced value of market quotes.  
\end{enumerate}
We run an optimiser to minimise the error between model induced quotes and market quotes.



\subsubsection{Nested calibration}%
We decouple the calibration of model parameters and that of the curves. This leads to the following algorithm.

Until convergence is achieved
\begin{enumerate}
\item Initialise the stochastic model:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Start with an initial guess for $\sigma^{f\to d}_\cdot$ and correlations.
\item Bootstrap the FX spot volatility $\sigma^{df}_\cdot$ to match FX options prices.
\end{enumerate}
\item Build the discount curve $Z^{f|d}_{t_0,\cdot}$ and index curve $L^{f|d}_{t_0,T;\theta}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute all the convexity adjustments.  
\item Solve for the curve parameters to match currency basis spreads.
\end{enumerate}
\item Use the Gaussian approximation to compute the quadratic covariation of currency basis spreads 
\begin{eqnarray}
  %\Cov_{t_0}\left( B^{f/d|d,r}_{t_0,T_i;M_k}, B^{f/d|d,r}_{t_0,T_j;M_\ell} \right)
  \int_{t_0}^{T_i\wedge T_j} d \left\langle B^{f/d|d,r}_{t_0,T_i;M_k}, B^{f/d|d,r}_{t_0,T_j;M_\ell} \right\rangle
  &=& \int_t^{T_i\wedge T_j}  \ldots \, dt 
\end{eqnarray}
as well as their covariance with other factors.
\item Update $\sigma^{f\to d}_\cdot$ to match historical variances, update correlations to match historical covariances.
\end{enumerate}

Note that the FX forward will move at each iteration. For simplicity, we could assume the FX vol quote do not.

The update (gradient descent) can be implemented in several ways: finite difference, adjoint differentiation etc\ldots




\newpage%
\subsection{Multi-currency model}%


[TODO: This seems really hard to do in closed form! Brute force is having a function that calibrates the $ed$ market and the $df$ market and computes the delivered vols on the cross-FX $ef$. Then run this function, seen as a function of correlations, though an optimiser to reduce the error between model delivered quotes and market observed vols.]

\subsubsection{Odd correlations}


So called odd-correlations such as EUR vs USDJPY correlations cannot be implied from market quotes because there are no liquid market instruments directly exposed to them. The lack of any natural economical interpretation increases the uncertainty associated with historical estimation. This makes this type of correlation unfit as model inputs. On the other hand, options on the FX cross EURUSD are liquid. One can then use a model to imply the odd-correlation EUR vs USDJPY from the natural ones:  EUR vs EURUSD and EUR vs EURJPY.

In the BS-LGM model, this is very easy to do because the ATM vol of the cross is equivalent to a constraint on the FX-FX correlation. 

In the LGCM model, we have many more unknown correlations. Can we still apply the same reasoning?

It seems like we need to bootstrap the FX-FX correlations and FX-CB correlations at the same time. 

\subsubsection{Triangle relationship under stochastic collateral}



%\subsection{Variance of the cross FX-forward}


Let us write $\Sigma^{df}_{t,T}$ for the volatility of the FX-forward process:
\begin{eqnarray}
  \frac{dx^{df|c}_{t,T}}{x^{df|c}_{t,T}} &=& \Sigma^{df}_{t,T} \cdot dW^{d|c,T}_{t} 
\end{eqnarray}
We saw that 
\begin{eqnarray}
  \Sigma^{df}_{t,T} &=& \Sigma^{d|d}_{t,T} - \Sigma^{f|f}_{t,T} - \Sigma^{f\to d}_{t,T}  + \sigma^{df}_t  
\end{eqnarray}
does not depend on the collateral. 

%\begin{coro}
It follows that the total variance of an FX option with maturity $T$ is:
\begin{eqnarray}
  \sigma^{df|c}_{\BS,t}(T)^2  (T-t)
  &=& \int_t^T \left| \Sigma^{d|d}_{u,T} - \Sigma^{f|f}_{u,T} +\Sigma^{d\to f}_{u,T}  + \sigma^{df}_u  \right|^2 du
\end{eqnarray}
regardless of the collateral\footnote{But its moneyness does depend on the collateral.}.
%\end{coro}



Then, from $x^{ef}_{t,T} = x^{df}_{t,T}/x^{de}_{t,T}$, we find 
\begin{eqnarray}
  \Sigma^{ef}_{t,T} &=& \Sigma^{df}_{t,T}  - \Sigma^{de}_{t,T}  .
\end{eqnarray}
It follows that implied vols of the cross-FX are given by:
\begin{eqnarray}
  \sigma^{ef}_{\BS,t}(T)^2  (T-t)
  &=& \int_t^T \left| \Sigma^{df}_{u,T}  \right|^2 \, du
+ \int_t^T \left| \Sigma^{de}_{u,T}  \right|^2 \, du
- 2 \int_t^T \Sigma^{df}_{u,T} \cdot \Sigma^{de}_{u,T} \,du 
\end{eqnarray}
This can be viewed as a constraint on the correlation between the two FX rates. 

When all factors are one-dimensional, assuming all other correlations are known, we can solve for the FX-FX spot correlation to match the delivered ATMF vols on the cross.


In the deterministic spread setting, one only has to mark IR-IR as well as FX-IRD and FX-IRF correlations. In this new context, one has to mark all collateral basis correlations as well. 

This requires to define SDP matrices. 
Note that synchronicity of time series is crucial for correlation estimations.

\subsubsection{Implied correlations}
We can generalise the reasoning to imply so-called odd-correlations by considering the covariance between the cross-FX and other assets.



\subsubsection{Application to off-market discount curves}%

What does the $Z^{\mathrm{EUR}|\mathrm{JPY}}_{t,T}$ curve look like? 

There is no market for cross-currency swaps collateralized in JPY. So we have to build it from the model calibrated to the EUR/JPY/USD market.

\newpage%
\section{Bonds, repo and repo convexity}\label{sec:repo}%

McCloud \cite{McCloud:RepoConvexity} explains the observed basis between repo discount factors, implied from repo rates, and bond discount factors, stripped from bond prices, as a convexity effect: the repo is valued with the derivative discount rate, while the bond delivered in the repo is priced with the bond discount rate. We show that this \emph{repo convexity} is a forward collateral convexity adjustment in the sense of \autoref{prop:collateral_convexity}, with the bond curve playing the role of the native curve and the derivative curve that of the collateral. The LGCM gives it in closed form for any number of factors, and McCloud's Hull--White formulas are the one factor case.

\subsection{Setting}

\begin{hypo}
Single currency, no default, no haircut. Under one risk-neutral measure, the \emph{bond curve} and the \emph{derivative curve} are
\begin{eqnarray}
	\bar p^T_t \;=\; \EE_t\left[ e^{-\int_t^T \bar r_u du} \right], \qquad
	p^T_t \;=\; \EE_t\left[ e^{-\int_t^T r_u du} \right], \qquad r_t \;=\; \bar r_t + b_t ,
\end{eqnarray}
where $\bar r$ is the bond discount rate, $r$ the derivative (OIS) discount rate and $b$ the \emph{discounting basis} (McCloud (11)). The volatilities are $d\bar p^T_t/\bar p^T_t = \bar r_t dt - \bar\Sigma_{t,T}\cdot dW_t$, $dp^T_t/p^T_t = r_t dt - \Sigma^{p}_{t,T}\cdot dW_t$, and $\Sigma^b_{t,T} := \Sigma^{p}_{t,T} - \bar\Sigma_{t,T}$. The forward measures of the two curves are $\bar\QQ^{T}$ and $\QQ^{p,T}$.
\end{hypo}

\begin{rema}[Bonds as collateral]
This is the structure of native and non-native collateral of \autoref{sec:market_observables_list}: $\bar p = Z^{f|f}$, $p = Z^{f|d}$, $b = q^{f\to d}$ and $\Sigma^b = \Sigma^{f\to d}$, with the convention $\Sigma^{d|j} = \Sigma^{d|i} + \Sigma^{i\to j}$. As noted in the footnote on non-cash collateral in \autoref{ssec:rn_pricing}, a rehypothecable security posted as collateral earns its repo rate: the bond curve is the curve of the bond as collateral. In the code, \texttt{repo.RepoModel} is an LGCM with the block $f$ for the bond curve, the block $c$ for the basis, no $d$ factor and zero FX volatility.
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[Liquidity basis]
McCloud splits the discount rate of the bond maturing at $T$ as $\bar r^T_t = \bar r_t + \bar z^T_t$ (curve and liquidity contributions, (9)) and defines the liquidity adjustment $L^{eT}_t = \log \bar\EE^{eT}_t[\exp\int_t^e \bar s^{eT}_u du]$, $\bar s^{eT} = \bar z^T - \bar z^e$, which is $\mu_S + \frac12\sigma_S^2$ for a Gaussian $S = \int_t^e \bar s^{eT}_u du$ ((13), (16), (21)). It multiplies the repo discount factors by $e^{-L^{eT}_t}$. We set $\bar z = 0$ in the rest of this section.
\end{rema}

\subsection{Repo contracts}

\begin{defi}[Repo]
A forward-starting repo agreed at $t$ over $[s,e]$ on the bond maturing at $T$, $t\le s<e\le T$, exchanges at $s$ one unit of cash for $1/\bar p^T_s$ bonds, which are returned at $e$ against $1+\delta f^{eT}_{ts}$. The \emph{repo rate} $f^{eT}_{ts}$ makes its value zero at $t$; the repo is discounted at $r$ (McCloud (10)).
\end{defi}

\begin{rema}[Time and day count]
Model time is in ACT/365 years: the interval $e - s$ drives the convexity. The accrual fraction $\delta$ only converts the gross forward price into a quoted rate. It is a parameter of the contract: $\delta = (e-s)\cdot 365/360$ for ACT/360 (USD, EUR repo) and $\delta = e - s$ for ACT/365 (GBP). The convexity $K^{\mathrm{repo}}$ below does not depend on $\delta$.
\end{rema}

\subsection{Repo rate as a collateral convexity adjustment}

\begin{prop}[Repo rate]\label{prop:repo_rate}
\begin{eqnarray}
	1 + \delta f^{eT}_{ts} &=& \frac{\bar p^s_t}{\bar p^e_t} \, K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(s,e,T), \qquad
	K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(s,e,T) \;:=\; \frac{\EE^{p,e}_t\left[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s\right]}{\bar\EE^{e}_t\left[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s\right]}
	\;=\; \Q^{\bar p\to p}_{t,e}\left[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s\right].
\end{eqnarray}
This is model-free.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
The zero value of the repo at $t$ is $\EE_t[e^{-\int_t^e r_u du}(\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s - (1+\delta f))] = 0$, so $1 + \delta f = \EE^{p,e}_t[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s]$. Under $\bar\QQ^e$, $\bar p^T/\bar p^e$ and $\bar p^s/\bar p^e$ are martingales, so $\bar\EE^e_s[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s] = 1/\bar p^e_s$ and $\bar\EE^e_t[1/\bar p^e_s] = \bar p^s_t/\bar p^e_t$. Divide.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Repo convexity in the LGCM}

\begin{prop}[LGCM repo convexity]\label{prop:repo_lgcm}
With deterministic volatilities,
\begin{eqnarray}
	\ln K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(s,e,T) &=& -\int_t^s \left(\bar\Sigma_{u,e} - \bar\Sigma_{u,s}\right)\cdot\Sigma^b_{u,e}\,du
	\;-\; \int_s^e \left(\bar\Sigma_{u,e} - \bar\Sigma_{u,T}\right)\cdot\Sigma^b_{u,e}\,du .
\end{eqnarray}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
$v_u := \bar\EE^e_u[\bar p^T_e/\bar p^T_s]$ is a $\bar\QQ^e$-martingale with $v_u = \bar p^s_u/\bar p^e_u$ for $u\le s$ and $v_u = \bar p^T_u/(\bar p^e_u\,\bar p^T_s)$ for $s\le u\le e$. It is lognormal with volatility $\sigma^v_u = \bar\Sigma_{u,e} - \bar\Sigma_{u,s}$ on $[t,s]$ and $\bar\Sigma_{u,e} - \bar\Sigma_{u,T}$ on $[s,e]$. Apply \autoref{prop:collateral_convexity} with $i\to j = \bar p\to p$ and maturity $e$.
\end{proof}

The first term is the Libor collateral convexity \eqref{eq:libor_collateral_convexity_lgcm} of the forward rate on $[s,e]$; it is the whole convexity for a repo to maturity ($T = e$). The second term is new: during the repo window the bond still has $T-e$ to run, and its price at $e$ is correlated with the basis.

\begin{coro}[Maturity and forwardness]\label{coro:repo_m_f}
Let $C^{eT}_{ts} = \ln K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(s,e,T)$, $M^{eT}_t = C^{eT}_{tt} = \int_t^e (\bar\Sigma_{u,T} - \bar\Sigma_{u,e})\cdot\Sigma^b_{u,e}\,du$ and $F^{eT}_{ts} = C^{eT}_{ts} - (M^{eT}_t - M^{sT}_t)$ (McCloud (17)). Then $M^{ee}_t = 0$, $F^{eT}_{tt} = 0$ and, with the repo discount factor $\hat p^{eT}_t = \bar p^e_t e^{-M^{eT}_t}$,
\begin{eqnarray}
	1 + \delta f^{eT}_{ts} &=& \frac{\hat p^{sT}_t}{\hat p^{eT}_t} e^{F^{eT}_{ts}} .
\end{eqnarray}
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
The definitions and \autoref{prop:repo_rate}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Hull--White case}

\begin{prop}[Hull--White repo convexity]\label{prop:repo_hw}
Let $d\bar r_t = \theta(\bar r^*_t - \bar r_t)dt + \sigma dx_t$ and $db_t = \kappa(b^*_t - b_t)dt + \varepsilon dy_t$ with $dx_t\,dy_t = \rho\,dt$ (McCloud (27)). With $E(\beta,\tau) = (1-e^{-\beta\tau})/\beta$, $\bar\Sigma_{u,T} = \sigma E(\theta, T-u)$ and $\Sigma^b_{u,T} = \varepsilon E(\kappa, T-u)$. Then
\begin{eqnarray}
	C^{eT}_{ts} &=& \frac{\rho\sigma\varepsilon}{\theta\kappa}\Big( (1-e^{-\theta(T-e)})\int_t^e e^{-\theta(e-u)}(1-e^{-\kappa(e-u)})du \nonumber\\
	&& \qquad - (1-e^{-\theta(T-s)})\int_t^s e^{-\theta(s-u)}(1-e^{-\kappa(e-u)})du \Big),
\end{eqnarray}
which is McCloud (30); integrating gives his (33) and (34).
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
$\bar\Sigma_{u,e} - \bar\Sigma_{u,s} = \sigma e^{-\theta(s-u)}E(\theta,e-s)$ and $\bar\Sigma_{u,T} - \bar\Sigma_{u,e} = \sigma e^{-\theta(e-u)}E(\theta,T-e)$. Substitute in \autoref{prop:repo_lgcm} and split $\int_t^e = \int_t^s + \int_s^e$ in (30).
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Check of McCloud's formulas]
McCloud's (30), (33) and (34) agree with \autoref{prop:repo_lgcm}, including the signs, with $\rho$ the correlation of $\bar r$ and $b$: the measured agreement is $10^{-11}$ relative or better. The closed forms divide by $\theta\kappa(\theta+\kappa)$. The LGCM formula is stable at zero mean reversion, where
\begin{eqnarray*}
	C^{eT}_{ts} &=& -\rho\sigma\varepsilon\left[\tfrac12(e-s)\left((e-t)^2-(e-s)^2\right) - \tfrac12(T-e)(e-s)^2\right] .
\end{eqnarray*}
Both checks are in \texttt{tests/test\_repo.py}.
\end{rema}

\subsection{Repo discount curves}

\begin{prop}[Hull--White repo curve]\label{prop:repo_hw_curve}
In the Hull--White case of \autoref{prop:repo_hw}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The infinite maturity repo curve $\hat p^{e}_t := \hat p^{e\infty}_t$ has instantaneous forwards $\hat f^e_t = \bar f^e_t + \frac{\rho\sigma\varepsilon}{\theta\kappa}e^{-\theta(e-t)}(1-e^{-\kappa(e-t)})$ (McCloud (38)). For $e\ge E$, $\hat f^e_t - \bar f^e_t = (\hat f^E_t - \bar f^E_t)\,e^{-\theta(e-E)}\frac{1-e^{-\kappa(e-t)}}{1-e^{-\kappa(E-t)}}$ (McCloud (39)).
\item $\hat p^{eT}_t = (\bar p^e_t)^{w}(\hat p^{e}_t)^{1-w}$ with $w = e^{-\theta(T-e)}$ (McCloud (41)).
\item $F^{eT}_{ts} = (1 - e^{-(\theta+\kappa)(s-t)})\,F^{eT}_{-\infty,s}$, so $1+\delta f^{eT}_{ts} = (1+\delta f^{eT}_{ss})^{w'}(1+\delta f^{eT}_{-\infty,s})^{1-w'}$ with $w' = e^{-(\theta+\kappa)(s-t)}$ at fixed $\hat p^{sT}_t/\hat p^{eT}_t$ (McCloud (43)).
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
By (34), $M^{eT}_t = (1-e^{-\theta(T-e)})M^{e\infty}_t$, which gives 2.; differentiate $M^{e\infty}_t$ in $e$ for 1.; $F^{eT}_{ts}$ in (34) has the factor $(1-e^{-(\theta+\kappa)(s-t)})$, and its other factors depend on $s$, $e$, $T$ only, which gives 3.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Beyond Hull--White]
The interpolations of \autoref{prop:repo_hw_curve} use a single mean reversion and do not hold with several factors or piecewise constant parameters. In a two-factor bond, one-factor basis piecewise constant model (\texttt{test\_interpolation\_fails\_beyond\_hull\_white}), the mean reversion implied by $M^{eT}/M^{e\infty} = 1-e^{-\theta(T-e)}$ is $0.0232$ at $T-e = 2$ and $0.0296$ at $T-e = 20$ (measured), and the ratio $F(\tau)/F(\infty)$ is $0.18$, $0.31$, $1.02$, $1.53$, $1.38$ at $\tau = 0.5, 1, 3, 6, 10$: not monotone and above one. Use \autoref{prop:repo_lgcm} directly.
\end{rema}

\subsection{Coupon bonds}

\begin{defi}[Repo on a coupon bond]
A coupon bond pays $c_k$ at $T_k$ and is worth $\bar P_t = \sum_{T_k>t} c_k\bar p^{T_k}_t$ (ex-coupon at the payment dates). In the repo over $[s,e]$, one unit of cash buys $1/\bar P_s$ bonds at $s$; the bond matures at or after $e$; the cash flows paid in $(s,e]$, including a redemption at $e$, are passed through to the bond's economic owner at $T_k$ (manufactured payments, discounted at $r$); a coupon at $s$ belongs to the seller; at $e$ the ex-coupon bonds are returned against $1+\delta f$.
\end{defi}

\begin{prop}[Coupon repo rate]\label{prop:repo_coupon}
\begin{eqnarray}
	p^e_t\,(1+\delta f) &=& p^s_t\,\EE^{p,s}_t[Y_s], \qquad
	Y_s \;=\; \frac{1}{\bar P_s}\left( \sum_{T_k>e} c_k\, p^e_s\,\frac{\bar p^{T_k}_s}{\bar p^e_s}\,K^{\mathrm{repo}}_s(s,e,T_k) + \sum_{s<T_k\le e} c_k\, p^{T_k}_s \right).
\end{eqnarray}
In the LGCM every term of $Y_s$ is the exponential of an affine function of the Gaussian state $z_s$, and $\EE^{p,s}[Y_s]$ is computed exactly by Gauss--Hermite quadrature. With a single cash flow it is \autoref{prop:repo_rate}. If $b = 0$, then $Y_s = 1$ and $1+\delta f = \bar p^s_t/\bar p^e_t$ for any coupons.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Condition on $\mathcal F_s$. By \autoref{prop:repo_rate} at $t = s$, $\EE^{p,e}_s[\bar p^{T_k}_e] = (\bar p^{T_k}_s/\bar p^e_s)K^{\mathrm{repo}}_s(s,e,T_k)$, and a coupon at $T_k\in(s,e]$ is worth $c_k p^{T_k}_s$ at $s$. If $b = 0$ then $p = \bar p$, $K = 1$ and the bracket is $\bar P_s$.
\end{proof}

\begin{defi}[Frozen weights]\label{defi:repo_frozen}
Write $Y_s = N(z)/D(z)$ with $N = \sum_k a_k e^{\alpha_k\cdot z}$, $D = \sum_j c_j e^{\beta_j\cdot z}$ and $z\sim\mathcal N(\mu, P)$ under $\QQ^{p,s}$. Replace $D$ by the lognormal with the same mean $\bar D = \sum_j c_j e^{\beta_j\cdot\mu + \frac12\beta_j^\top P\beta_j}$ and the loading $\bar\beta = \sum_j w_j\beta_j$, $w_j = c_j e^{\beta_j\cdot\mu + \frac12\beta_j^\top P\beta_j}/\bar D$. This gives, for any sum of exponentials $X = \sum_k x_k e^{\gamma_k\cdot z}$,
\begin{eqnarray}
	\mathrm{FW}[X] &:=& \frac{1}{\bar D}\sum_k x_k \exp\left(\gamma_k\cdot\mu + \tfrac12\bar\beta^\top P\bar\beta + \tfrac12(\gamma_k-\bar\beta)^\top P(\gamma_k-\bar\beta)\right) \;\approx\; \EE\left[X/D\right] .
\end{eqnarray}
The frozen-weights approximation of the coupon repo rate uses $\EE[D/D] = 1$ as a control:
\begin{eqnarray}
	\EE^{p,s}[Y_s] &\approx& 1 + \mathrm{FW}[N] - \mathrm{FW}[D] .
\end{eqnarray}
\end{defi}

\begin{rema}[Accuracy of frozen weights]\label{rema:repo_frozen}
The control makes the approximation exact when $N = D$, in particular for $b = 0$ (\autoref{prop:repo_coupon}), and for a single cash flow, where $\mathrm{FW}$ is exact. Without the control, $\mathrm{FW}[D] = \sum_j w_j e^{\bar\beta^\top P\bar\beta - \bar\beta^\top P\beta_j} \neq 1$, which breaks the $b=0$ identity; at the parameters of \autoref{rema:repo_verification} this artefact was $96\%$ of the approximation error (6\% coupon, window $[1.2,1.6]$). Measured with the control: the error of the repo rate scales as $\lambda^4$ when all volatilities are scaled by $\lambda$ (errors $2.6\cdot10^{-5}$, $1.6\cdot10^{-6}$, $1.0\cdot10^{-7}$ for $\lambda = 4, 2, 1$, ratios $16.2$ and $16.0$). This is an observation, not a proof. For a $10$Y semi-annual bond with a coupon in the window $[1.2,1.6]$, the approximation misses at most $0.56\%$ of the gap between the repo rate and the bond forward (coupons $0$ to $8\%$).
\end{rema}

\begin{rema}[What the coupon adds]
For a coupon bond, the gap between the repo rate and the bond forward is not all convexity. A coupon in $(s,e]$ is passed through at $T_k$ and discounted at $r$, while the bonds are priced at $\bar r$: with $b \neq 0$ this moves the rate even at $\rho = 0$. In the example above, at a $6\%$ coupon, the gap is $-0.124$bp at $\rho = 0$ and a further $-0.057$bp from the correlation; for a zero-coupon bond it is all correlation ($-0.143$bp). The notebook plots the two parts separately.
\end{rema}

\subsection{Bond-collateralised derivatives}

\begin{prop}[Overnight repo]\label{prop:repo_overnight}
In the LGCM, as $h\to0$: $\ln K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(t,t+h,T) = O(h^2)$, so the spot overnight repo rate on any bond of the curve converges to $\bar r_t$. For a forward start $s>t$,
\begin{eqnarray}
	\frac1h \ln K^{\mathrm{repo}}_t(s,s+h,T) &\to& -\int_t^s \partial_s\bar\Sigma_{u,s}\cdot\Sigma^b_{u,s}\,du ,
\end{eqnarray}
which is $-\rho\sigma\varepsilon\,(E(\theta,s-t)-E(\theta+\kappa,s-t))/\kappa$ in the Hull--White case: the forward overnight repo rate is not the bond forward rate.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
For $s = t$ the first integral of \autoref{prop:repo_lgcm} vanishes; in the second, $\bar\Sigma_{u,t+h} - \bar\Sigma_{u,T}$ is bounded and $\Sigma^b_{u,t+h} = O(h)$ on an interval of length $h$. For $s > t$, $\bar\Sigma_{u,s+h} - \bar\Sigma_{u,s} = h\,\partial_s\bar\Sigma_{u,s} + O(h^2)$ and the second integral is $O(h^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{hypo}[Security collateral]
Posting a fully rehypothecable security as collateral, with no haircut, is equivalent to posting cash collateral that earns the security's spot overnight repo rate: the effective discount rate of the collateralised trade is that repo rate. This is the extension to non-cash collateral suggested in the footnote of \autoref{ssec:rn_pricing} (see also Piterbarg \cite{Piterbarg:Cooking}); it is an assumption on how the collateral is financed, not a consequence of the framework.
\end{hypo}

\begin{prop}[Discount curve of bond-collateralised derivatives]\label{prop:repo_bond_collateral}
Under the Hypothesis "Security collateral", a derivative collateralised by a bond of the curve (no haircut, $\bar z = 0$) has effective discount rate $\bar r$: its discount curve is the bond curve $\bar p$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
By \autoref{prop:repo_overnight}, the spot overnight repo rate of the bond at $u$ is $\bar r_u$. By the Hypothesis, $V_t = \EE_t[e^{-\int_t^T\bar r_u du}V_T]$, and $V_T = 1$ gives $\bar p^T_t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rema}[Term repo curve or bond curve]
McCloud (\S4) proposes the extrapolated term repo curve $\hat p^e$ for discounting bond-collateralised derivatives. That corresponds to a different financing assumption than the Hypothesis "Security collateral": the collateral financed with term repos locked at inception. Under rolling overnight financing, \autoref{prop:repo_bond_collateral} gives $\bar p$, and the two differ by the maturity adjustment $M^{e\infty}$. Which one applies is a question about how the collateral is actually funded, not one the model decides. The size of the difference is shown in the notebook \texttt{repo\_convexity.py} (in \texttt{python/notebooks}).
\end{rema}

\subsection{Verification}

\begin{rema}[Verification]\label{rema:repo_verification}
The checks are in \texttt{python/convexity/tests/test\_repo.py}. Hull--White parameters are $\theta = 5\%$, $\sigma = 1\%$, $\kappa = 40\%$, $\varepsilon = 0.8\%$, $\rho = -0.5$, with flat curves $\bar r = 3.5\%$, $b = -20$bp.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \autoref{prop:repo_lgcm} against nested adaptive quadrature in a two-factor bond, one-factor basis piecewise model with zero, tiny and negative mean reversions: $6\cdot10^{-15}$ relative. For $T = e$ it equals the Libor collateral convexity to $10^{-16}$ absolute (the round-off of \texttt{libor\_convexity}; $1.5\cdot10^{-11}$ relative at $\ln K = 5\cdot10^{-6}$).
\item \autoref{prop:repo_hw}: McCloud's closed forms to $10^{-11}$ relative, also past the last knot and for small and negative mean reversions; the polynomial limit at $\theta = \kappa = 0$ to $10^{-12}$.
\item \autoref{prop:repo_hw_curve}: the interpolations (41), (43) and the extrapolation (39) hold to $10^{-12}$ in Hull--White; the forward spread (38) matches a central finite difference of $M^{e\infty}_0$ to $10^{-7}$; the failure beyond Hull--White is quoted in the Remark above.
\item \autoref{prop:repo_coupon}: a one-cash-flow bond reproduces \autoref{prop:repo_rate} to $10^{-13}$; $b = 0$ gives the bond forward to $10^{-13}$; Gauss--Hermite with $40$ and $60$ nodes agree to $10^{-14}$.
\item Monte Carlo (exact LGCM scheme, $2^{18}$ antithetic paths, seeds $1$ to $4$, volatilities scaled by $3$, control variates $\EE[D_{0,e}] = p^e_0$ and the bond-collateralised repo of value $\bar p^s_0$): the zero-coupon repo rate ($s=1.3$, $e=2.1$, $T=7.5$, convexity $-1.20$bp) is at $z = -1.39, +0.57, +0.45, -0.23$; the bond forward ($K = 1$) is rejected at $7.8$ to $9.8$ standard errors. The coupon repo rate (10Y semi-annual 5\%, two coupons in the window) is at $z = -0.88, +1.42, +0.72, -0.57$ against the quadrature; the frozen weights approximation (Definition~\ref{defi:repo_frozen}) is also within $4$ standard errors ($z = -1.41, +0.89, +0.19, -1.10$).
\item \autoref{prop:repo_overnight}: $\ln K_0(0,h,10)$ scales as $h^2$; the forward overnight limit matches the Hull--White closed form ($1.14$bp at $s = 3$).
\end{enumerate}
\end{rema}
